Distributional Off-policy Evaluation with Bellman Residual Minimization
Sungee Hong, Zhengling Qi, Raymond K. W. Wong

Abstract
We study distributional off-policy evaluation (OPE), of which the goal is to learn the distribution of the return for a target policy using offline data generated by a different policy. The theoretical foundation of many existing work relies on the supremum-extended statistical distances such as supremum-Wasserstein distance, which are hard to estimate. In contrast, we study the more manageable expectation-extended statistical distances and provide a novel theoretical justification on their validity for learning the return distribution. Based on this attractive property, we propose a new method called Energy Bellman Residual Minimizer (EBRM) for distributional OPE. We provide corresponding in-depth theoretical analyses. We establish a finite-sample error bound for the EBRM estimator under the realizability assumption. Furthermore, we introduce a variant of our method based on a multi-step extension which improves the error bound for non-realizable settings. Notably, unlike prior distributional OPE methods, the theoretical guarantees of our method do not require the completeness assumption.



1
INTRODUCTION

In reinforcement learning (RL), the cumulative sum of (discounted) reward, also known as the return, is a crucial quantity for evaluating the performance of a target policy. While traditional RL focus on the expectation of the return, distributional RL (DRL) relies on the whole distribution.
Since
Bellemare
et al. 2017
first introduced a DRL algorithm
and showed that it outperformed Deep-Q Network
(
Mnih
et al. 2015
)
(based on traditional RL) in Atari games, DRL has gained significant interest and has already been successfully applied in a variety of areas
(
Bodnar et al. 2019
;
Bellemare et al. 2020
;
Wurman
et al. 2022
;
Fawzi
et al. 2022
, e.g.,)
.

In this paper, we consider the problem of off-policy evaluation (OPE) in DRL under infinite-horizon settings.
The underlying evaluation task is based on offline data generated by a policy that is possibly different from the target policy.
In contrast to traditional OPE, distributional OPE aims to learn the distribution of the return instead of merely its expectation.
Despite its challenging nature,
the whole return distribution offers much richer information than its expectation
and is useful in many ways.

Rowland et al. 2023
and
Lyle
et al. 2019
demonstrated that their estimation of distribution can outperform traditional RL even in expectation-related tasks (e.g., estimation and maximization of expected return) under appropriate conditions. Furthermore, by taking into account different distributional attributes, one can study and build risk-sensitive policies
(
Dabney
et al. 2018
;
Ma
et al. 2021
, e.g.,)
, and perform risk assessment
(
Huang et al. 2022
)
. Several recent theoretical studies have been conducted on risk-sensitive distributional learning
(
Keramati et al. 2020
;
Liang and
Luo 2024
;
Chen
et al. 2024
, e.g.,)
.

1.1
Expectation-extension For Distributional OPE

Early DRL methods
(
Bellemare
et al. 2017
;
Dabney
et al. 2018
;
Nguyen-Tang
et al. 2021
, e.g.,)

are motivated by

supremum-extended
distances (see (
3
) below) due to its contraction property.
However, the supremum-extended distance is based on taking supremum over all state-action pairs, and hence difficult to be controlled well based on data, especially for non-tabular settings that have
infinite (and possibly uncountable) number of state-action pairs.
So, in this paper, we resort to the weaker
expectation-extended
distances (see (
5
)), which allows us to form the objective function in a more tractable way.
As a key result, we provide theoretical justifications of how these expectation-extended objective functions can lead to low inaccuracy (See Theorem
2.2
of Section
2
) for OPE.
Our result can also be useful for understanding the practical success of many existing methods, especially in non-tabular settings. Based on expectation-extended distance, we have developed a new method for distributional OPE based on energy distance, which is closely related to maximum mean discrepancy (MMD). We elaborate some key differences between our work and a prior work called MMDRL
(
Nguyen-Tang
et al. 2021
)
in Section
3.1
, and demonstrate the superior numerical performance of our method in Section
5
.

1.2
Statistical Error Bound

A key feature of our work is the in-depth analysis of finite-sample error bounds (Sections
3
and
4
).
To date, there are very few theoretical statistical analysis for infinite-horizontal setting.
Several early papers of DRL
(
Bellemare
et al. 2017
;
Dabney
et al. 2018
, e.g.,)
only proved contraction of the (true) distributional Bellman operator with respect to different distances.

Rowland et al. 2019
and
Marthe
et al. 2024
showed that a finite number of statistics could be learned accurately through iterations via the true Bellman operator.
However, the nature of these theories does not take into account the estimation errors based on data.
Only a few prior works in DRL showed statistical convergence for OPE.
Rowland et al. 2018
;
Rowland
et al. 2023
established almost-sure convergence
of Categorical algorithm and QRTD, but no error bound is provided.
Ma
et al. 2021
derived a statistical error bound for their method, but its theory neglects the fact that practical models cannot encompass all distributions.
Peng
et al. 2024
has provided a statistical error bound for several models, but they require generative model.
Without these stringent modeling assumptions,
two recent works, FLE
(
Wu
et al. 2023
)
and DISCO
(
Wang
et al. 2023
)
, also provided error bound analyses, but they rely on another common but strong assumption called “completeness,” which will be discussed in Section
1.3
below.
Furthermore, most methods are restricted to tabular settings that have finite number of state-action pairs (Table
5
of Appendix
D.1
).
In this paper, we will conduct our theoretical analysis with both tabular and non-tabular settings under realizability assumption (i.e., no model-misspecification error), as well as non-realizability.

1.3
Relaxing Completeness

One notable advantage of our analysis (and our estimator) is
that we do not require a completeness assumption, as required in
recent works such as FLE
(
Wu
et al. 2023
)
and DISCO
(
Wang
et al. 2023
)
.
Simply put, completeness requires that the distributional Bellman operator maps any element in the assumed model back into the model.
Although the completeness assumption has been routinely assumed in many theoretical analyses in traditional RL, it is a strong assumption.

Chen and
Jiang 2019
pointed out that completeness is not monotonic. That is, unlike realizability, enlarging the model to encompass more possible distributions may break completeness. Not only is it tricky to satisfy, but its violation can also lead to convergence toward a fixed point that is distant from the true target
(
Kolter 2011
;
Tsitsiklis and
Van Roy 1996
;
Munos and
Szepesvari 2008
, e.g.,)
. (Our simulation illustrates similar issues for DRL in Column 3 of Figure
10
of Appendix
D.7
.)
Given the stringent and nontrivial nature of the completeness assumption, even in traditional RL, recent attempts have been made to relax completeness to realizability
(
Perdomo et al. 2023
;
Zanette 2023
, e.g.,)
. We remark that a series of work based on DICE
(
Nachum
et al. 2019
;
Zhang
et al. 2020
, e.g.,)
do not rely on the completeness assumption, but they are developed for traditional RL.
Table
1
shows the comparison of important theoretical aspects among different distributional OPE methods.

In Section
3
, we analyze our estimator under realizability (but no completeness assumption). In Section
4
,
we also extent our method to non-realizability settings and propose multi-step extensions to alleviate
the issues caused by misspecification.

1.4
Summary

We summarize our contributions as follows. (1) We provide a theoretical foundation for the application of the expectation-extended distance for distributional OPE. See Section
2
.
(2) We develop a novel distributional OPE method called EBRM, together with its finite-sample error bound. See Section
3
. (3) We further develop a multi-step extension of EBRM for non-realizabile settings in Section
4
.
(4) Our numerical experiments in Section
5
demonstrate strong performance of EBRM compared to some baseline methods.

2
OFF-POLICY EVALUATION BASED ON BELLMAN EQUATION

2.1
Background

We consider a distributional off-policy evaluation (OPE) problem within the framework of infinite-horizon Markov Decision Process (MDP),
which is characterized by a state space
\mathcal{S}
, a discrete action space
\mathcal{A}
, and
a transition kernel
p:\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{S})
with
d\geq 1
and
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})
denoting the class of probability measures over a generic space
\mathcal{X}
. In other words,
p
defines a joint distribution of a
d
-dimensional immediate reward and the next state conditioned on a state-action pair.
At each time point, an action is chosen by the agent based on the current state according to some (stochastic) policy, which is a mapping from
\mathcal{S}
to
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{A})
.
A trajectory generated by such an MDP can be written as
\{S^{(t)},A^{(t)},R^{(t+1)}\}_{t\geq 0}
. The return variable is defined as
Z:=\sum_{t=1}^{\infty}\gamma^{t-1}R^{(t)}
with
\gamma\in[0,1)
being a discount factor, based on which we can evaluate the performance of some target policy
\pi
.

OPE is different from on-policy evaluation in that the data are collected using a different policy called behavior policy
b
, other than the target policy
\pi
that we want to evaluate. Therefore, OPE naturally involves an issue of distributional shift.
Traditional OPE methods are mainly focused on estimating the expectation of the return
Z
under the target policy
\pi
, while DRL aims to estimate the whole distribution of
Z
.
In DRL,
letting
\mathcal{L}(X)
be the probability measure of some random variable (or vector)
X
, our target is to estimate the collection of return distributions conditioned on different initial state-action pairs
(S^{(0)},A^{(0)})=(s,a)
:

\displaystyle\Upsilon_{\pi}(s,a):=\mathcal{L}\bigg(\sum_{t=1}^{\infty}\gamma^{t-1}R^{(t)}\bigg),\text{ where }

\displaystyle(R^{(t+1)},S^{(t+1)})\sim p(\cdot|S^{(t)},A^{(t)}),\ A^{(t+1)}\sim\pi(\cdot|S^{(t+1)}),

collectively written as
\Upsilon_{\pi}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathcal{S\times A}}
.
It is analogous to the
Q
-function in traditional RL, whose evaluation at a state-action pair
(s,a)
is the expectation of the distribution
\Upsilon_{\pi}(s,a)
.
In this paper, the return variable is allowed to be multivariate (i.e.,
d>1
). For distributional evaluation, the multivariate extension is meaningful as the dependence between different dimensions of return can be assessed.

Similar to most existing DRL methods, our proposal is
based on the distributional Bellman equation
(
Bellemare
et al. 2017
)
.
Define the distributional Bellman operator by

\mathcal{T}^{\pi}:\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathcal{S\times A}}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathcal{S\times A}}

such that, for any
\Upsilon\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathcal{S\times A}}
and
s,a\in\mathcal{S\times A}
,

\displaystyle\big(\mathcal{T}^{\pi}\Upsilon\big)(s,a):=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{S\times A}}(g_{r,\gamma})_{\#}\Upsilon(s^{\prime},a^{\prime})\mathrm{d}\pi(a^{\prime}|s^{\prime})\mathrm{d}p(r,s^{\prime}|s,a),

(1)

where
(g_{r,\gamma})_{\#}:\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
maps the distribution of any random vector
X
to the distribution of
r+\gamma X
.
One can show that
\Upsilon_{\pi}
solves the distributional Bellman equation:

\mathcal{T}^{\pi}\Upsilon=\Upsilon,

(2)

with respect to
\Upsilon
. Due to the distributional Bellman equation (
2
), a sensible approach to finding
\Upsilon_{\pi}
is based on minimizing the discrepancy between
\mathcal{T}^{\pi}\Upsilon
and
\Upsilon
with respect to
\Upsilon\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathcal{S\times A}}
, which will be called
Bellman residual
hereafter.
To proceed with this approach, two important issues need to be addressed.
First, both
\mathcal{T}^{\pi}\Upsilon
and
\Upsilon
are collections of distributions over
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, based on which Bellman residual shall be quantified.
Second,
\mathcal{T}^{\pi}
may not be available and therefore needs to be estimated using data.
We will first focus on the quantification of Bellman residual, and defer the proposed estimator of
\mathcal{T}^{\pi}
and the formal description of our estimator for
\Upsilon_{\pi}
to Section
3
.

2.2
Existing Measures Of Bellman Residuals

To quantify the discrepancy between the two sides of the distributional Bellman equation (
2
), a common strategy is to start by selecting a statistical distance
\eta(\cdot,\cdot):\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\rightarrow[0,\infty]
,
and then define an extended-distance over
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathcal{S\times A}}

through combining the statistical distances over all state-action pairs. As shown in Table
4
in Appendix
D.1
, most existing methods
(
Bellemare
et al. 2017
;
Dabney
et al. 2018
;
Nguyen-Tang
et al. 2021
, e.g.,)

are theoretically based on some
supremum-extended
distance
\eta_{\infty}
defined as

\displaystyle\eta_{\infty}(\Upsilon_{1},\Upsilon_{2}):=\sup_{s,a}\eta\bigg\{\Upsilon_{1}(s,a),\Upsilon_{2}(s,a)\bigg\}.

(3)

Under various choices of
\eta
including Wasserstein-
p
metric with
1\leq p\leq\infty

(
Bellemare
et al. 2017
;
Dabney
et al. 2018
)
and maximum mean discrepancy
(
Nguyen-Tang
et al. 2021
)
,
it is shown that
\mathcal{T}^{\pi}

is a contraction with respect to
\eta_{\infty}
.
More specifically,
\eta_{\infty}(\mathcal{T}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{1},\mathcal{T}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{2})\leq\gamma^{\beta_{0}}\eta_{\infty}(\Upsilon_{1},\Upsilon_{2})
holds for any
\Upsilon_{1},\Upsilon_{2}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathcal{S\times A}}
, where the value of
\beta_{0}>0
depends on the choice of
\eta
.
If
\eta_{\infty}
is a metric, then the contractive property implies, for any
\Upsilon\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathcal{S\times A}}
,

\displaystyle\eta_{\infty}(\Upsilon,\Upsilon_{\pi})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\eta_{\infty}\big\{(\mathcal{T}^{\pi})^{k-1}\Upsilon,(\mathcal{T}^{\pi})^{k}\Upsilon\big\}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{\beta_{0}}}\cdot\eta_{\infty}(\Upsilon,\mathcal{T}^{\pi}\Upsilon).

(4)

As such, minimizing Bellman residual measured by
\eta_{\infty}
would be a sensible approach for finding
\Upsilon_{\pi}
. However, when
\mathcal{S\times A}
is very large (e.g., when either state or action is continuous), it is very difficult to estimate or control
\eta_{\infty}
by using offline data and also optimize with respect to
\Upsilon
.

It is more manageable to minimize Bellman residual with
expectation-extended
distance defined by

\displaystyle\bar{\eta}(\Upsilon_{1},\Upsilon_{2}):=\mathbb{E}_{(S,A)\sim b_{\mu}}\eta\bigg\{\Upsilon_{1}(S,A),\Upsilon_{2}(S,A)\bigg\},

(5)

with
(S,A)\sim b_{\mu}
.
Here
b_{\mu}=\mu\times b
refers to the offline data distribution over
\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}
induced by the behavior policy
b
.
With a slight abuse of the notation, we will overload the notation
b_{\mu}
with its density when there is no confusion.

We remark that (
4
) does not hold under
\bar{\eta}
because
\eta_{\infty}
and
\bar{\eta}
are not necessarily equivalent for
non-tabular state-action spaces (see Appendix
C.2.1
) which necessitates development of new theoretical foundation. Theoretical analysis has already been done on expectation-based objective functions in traditional RL
(
Antos
et al. 2008
;
Munos and
Szepesvari 2008
, e.g.,)
, and we hereby extend it to DRL.

2.3
Expectation-extended Distance

The fundamental question regarding the use of expectation-extended distances is:

Does minimizing expectation-extended Bellman residual lead to recovery of
\Upsilon_{\pi}
?

In off-policy setting, our target distribution is associated with the target policy
\pi
, which is different from the behavior policy
b
that generates the data
\mathcal{D}
. Due to this mismatch, the answer to the above question is not trivial.

To proceed, we shall focus on settings where state-action pairs of interest can be well covered by
b_{\mu}
, as formally stated in the following assumption. Let
q^{\pi}(s,a|\tilde{s},\tilde{a})
be the conditional probability density of the next state-action pair at
(s,a)
conditioned on the current state-action pair at
(\tilde{s},\tilde{a})
, induced by the transition probability
p
and the target policy
\pi
.

Assumption 2.1
.

\sup_{\tilde{s},\tilde{a},s,a}q^{\pi}(s,a|\tilde{s},\tilde{a})/b_{\mu}(s,a)<\infty
.

We note that every OPE method, regardless of traditional (non-distributional) or distributional RL, requires a form of coverage assumption. See Assumption 4.10 of
Wu
et al. 2023
and Section 5.5 of
Sutton and
Barto 2018
for examples. A trivial example that satisfies our Assumption
2.1
is when the denominator and the numerator are lower bounded and upper bounded, i.e.
p_{\rm min}:=\inf_{s,a}b_{\mu}(s,a)>0
and
p_{\rm max}:=\sup_{(s,a),(\tilde{s},\tilde{a})\in\mathcal{S\times A}}q^{\pi}(s,a|\tilde{s},\tilde{a})<\infty
.

In the following Theorem
2.2
(proved in Appendix
A.2
), we provide a solid ground for Bellman residual minimization based on expectation-extended distances.

Theorem 2.2
.

Under Assumption
2.1
, if the statistical distance
\eta
satisfies translation-invariance, scale-sensitivity, convexity, and relaxed triangular inequality defined in Appendix
A.2.1
, then we can bound the expectation-based inaccuracy: for any
\Upsilon\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathcal{S\times A}}
,

\bar{\eta}(\Upsilon,\Upsilon_{\pi})\leq B(\gamma)\bar{\eta}(\Upsilon,\mathcal{T}^{\pi}\Upsilon),

(6)

where
B(\gamma)
does not depend on
\Upsilon
and
B(\gamma)<\infty
for all
0\leq\gamma<1
. The precise bound and the rate of
B(\gamma)
(as
\gamma\rightarrow 1
) can be found in (
21
) and (
22
) of the Appendix.

Inequality (
6
) provides an analogy to Bound (
4
) for expectation-based distances, answering our prior question positively for multiple expectation-extended distances, including the examples listed in (
23
) of Appendix
A.3
.
Unlike tabular cases where
\bar{\eta}
and
\eta_{\infty}
can be viewed as equivalent, this is not a trivial result when applied to general state-action spaces, including the continuous state-action space, as explained in Appendix
C.2.1
.
In its full generality, Theorem
2.2
provides a foundation for estimators (not only ours) developed under expectation-based distance for settings with
general
(e.g., continuous) state-action spaces.

For solid construction and theoretical analysis, we will focus on a specific statistical distance.
In below we will construct our estimator based on
energy distance
(
Szekely and
Rizzo 2013
)
, which is defined as

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}\{\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{X}),\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{Y})\}:=

(7)

\displaystyle 2\mathbb{E}\|\mathbf{X}-\mathbf{Y}\|-\mathbb{E}\|\mathbf{X}-\mathbf{X}^{\prime}\|-\mathbb{E}\|\mathbf{Y}-\mathbf{Y}^{\prime}\|.

Here,
\mathbf{X}^{\prime}
and
\mathbf{Y}^{\prime}
are independent copies of
\mathbf{X}
and
\mathbf{Y}
respectively,
and
\mathbf{X},\mathbf{X}^{\prime},\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}^{\prime}
are independent. Energy distance can be computed easily even when we are given with multi-dimensional return
d\geq 2
and without closed-form density, which makes it more advantageous to other examples in (
23
).

3
ENERGY BELLMAN RESIDUAL MINIMIZER

3.1
Estimated Bellman Residual

Despite the applicability of Theorem
2.2
to general state-action space, we mainly focus on developing an estimator for the following two specialized settings: (1) tabular case; (2) continuous state-action space with deterministic transition (i.e. each
s,a
yields a deterministic
r,s^{\prime}
). Due to space limitation, we hereafter only focus on the tabular setting, leaving the setting with the continuous state-action space in Appendix
A.9
. We will present an in-depth theoretical study under both realizable and non-realizable settings in Sections
3.2
,
4.2
, and
4.3
.

Our target objective of Bellman residual minimization is

\displaystyle\bar{\mathcal{E}}(\Upsilon,\mathcal{T}^{\pi}\Upsilon)=\sum_{s,a}b_{\mu}(s,a)\cdot\mathcal{E}\big\{\Upsilon(s,a),\mathcal{T}^{\pi}\Upsilon(s,a)\big\}

(8)

with the term
\mathcal{E}\{\Upsilon(s,a),\mathcal{T}^{\pi}\Upsilon(s,a)\}
being defined as

\displaystyle 2\mathbb{E}\|Z_{\alpha}(s,a)-Z_{\beta}^{(1)}(s,a)\|-\mathbb{E}\|Z_{\alpha}(s,a)-Z_{\beta}(s,a)\|

\displaystyle-\mathbb{E}\|Z_{\alpha}^{(1)}(s,a)-Z_{\beta}^{(1)}(s,a)\|,

with fixed
s,a
. Here, the four random vectors
Z_{\alpha}(s,a),Z_{\beta}(s,a)\sim\Upsilon(s,a)
and
Z_{\alpha}^{(1)}(s,a)
,
Z_{\beta}^{(1)}(s,a)\sim\mathcal{T}^{\pi}\Upsilon(s,a)

are all independent. For the tabular case with offline data, we can estimate
b_{\mu}
and the transition
p
simply by empirical distributions. That is, given independent and identically distributed (i.i.d.) observations
\mathcal{D}=\{(s_{i},a_{i},r_{i},s_{i}^{\prime})\}_{i=1}^{N}
(with
s_{i},a_{i}\sim b_{\mu}
and
r_{i},s_{i}^{\prime}\sim p(\cdots|s_{i},a_{i})
), we consider

\displaystyle\hat{b}_{\mu}(s,a):=\frac{N(s,a)}{N},\ N(s,a):=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbf{1}\big\{(s_{i},a_{i})=(s,a)\big\},

(9)

and use the empirical probability measure
\hat{p}(E|s,a)
defined as follows for any measurable set
E
,

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\frac{1}{N(s,a)}\sum_{i:(s_{i},a_{i})=(s,a)}\delta_{r_{i},s_{i}^{\prime}}(E)&\text{if }N(s,a)\geq 1\\
\delta_{\mathbf{0},s}(E)&\text{if }N(s,a)=0\end{cases}

(10)

where
\delta_{r,s^{\prime}}
is the Dirac measure at
(r,s^{\prime})
. Based on this, we can estimate
\mathcal{T}^{\pi}
for any
\Upsilon\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathcal{S\times A}}
by the estimated transition
\hat{p}
and the target policy
\pi
, i.e., replacing
p
of (
1
) with
\hat{p}
.

Denoting the conditional expectation by
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}(\cdots):=\mathbb{E}(\cdots|\mathcal{D})
, we can compute
\mathcal{E}\{\Upsilon(s,a),\hat{\mathcal{T}}^{\pi}\Upsilon(s,a)\}
as

\displaystyle 2\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\|Z_{\alpha}(s,a)-\hat{Z}_{\beta}^{(1)}(s,a)\|-\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\|Z_{\alpha}(s,a)-Z_{\beta}(s,a)\|

\displaystyle-\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\|\hat{Z}_{\alpha}^{(1)}(s,a)-\hat{Z}_{\beta}^{(1)}(s,a)\|,

(11)

where the four
Z_{\alpha}(s,a),Z_{\beta}(s,a)\sim\Upsilon_{\theta}(s,a)
and
\hat{Z}_{\alpha}^{(1)}(s,a),\hat{Z}_{\beta}^{(1)}(s,a)\sim\hat{\mathcal{T}}^{\pi}\Upsilon(s,a)
are all independent conditioned on the observed data
\mathcal{D}
that determine
\hat{\mathcal{T}}^{\pi}
via
\hat{p}
. With the above construction, we can estimate the objective function as follows.

\hat{\bar{\mathcal{E}}}(\Upsilon,\hat{\mathcal{T}}^{\pi}\Upsilon)=\sum_{s,a}\hat{b}_{\mu}(s,a)\cdot\mathcal{E}\big\{\Upsilon(s,a),\hat{\mathcal{T}}^{\pi}\Upsilon(s,a)\big\}

(12)

Now letting
\{\Upsilon_{\theta}:\theta\in\Theta\}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathcal{S\times A}}
be the hypothesis class of
\Upsilon_{\pi}
,
where each distribution
\Upsilon_{\theta}
is indexed by an element of candidate space
\Theta
, a special case of which is the parametric case
\Theta\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{p}
. Then the proposed estimator of
\Upsilon_{\pi}
is

\Upsilon_{\hat{\theta}}
where

\hat{\theta}\in\arg\min_{\theta\in\Theta}\hat{\bar{\mathcal{E}}}(\Upsilon_{\theta},\hat{\mathcal{T}}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{\theta}).

(13)

We call our method the
Energy Bellman Residual Minimizer
(EBRM) and summarize it in Algorithm
1
of Appendix
A.4
. We will refer to the approach here as EBRM-single-step, as opposed to the multi-step extension EBRM-multi-step in Section
4.3
.

In passing, we would like to mention that energy distances have been used in the construction of a prior DRL method named MMDRL
(
Nguyen-Tang
et al. 2021
)
, but
there are significant differences between MMDRL and the proposed EBRM.
An important difference is that
EBRM minimizes with respect to
\theta
in
both
entries of (
13
) that correspond to LHS and RHS of the distributional Bellman equation, whereas MMDRL fixes the RHS with the previous parameter update in every iteration.
As shown in numerical experiments (Table
2
), EBRM performed significantly better than MMDRL.
Moreover, MMDRL does not provide any theoretical guarantee for its statistical performance, while we derived the statistical error bound for EBRM (Theorem
3.3
), as well as its multi-step extension (Section
4
).

3.2
Statistical Error Bound

In this subsection, we will provide a statistical error bound for EBRM-single-step.
We will first focus on the realizable setting and defer the analysis for the non-realizable case to Section
4
.

Assumption 3.1
.

The target
\Upsilon_{\pi}
can be realized by our candidate space
\Theta
. That is,
\Upsilon_{\pi}\in\mathcal{P}_{\Theta}:=\{\Upsilon_{\theta}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathcal{S\times A}}\ |\ \theta\in\Theta\}
.

This realizability assumption is already weaker than the completeness assumption, which is commonly used in theoretical analyses for distributional RL
(
Wu
et al. 2023
;
Wang
et al. 2023
, e.g.,)
and traditional RL
(
Chen and
Jiang 2019
;
Fan
et al. 2020
, e.g.,)
. Under completeness,
for all
\theta\in\Theta
, there exists a
\theta^{\prime}\in\Theta
such that
\mathcal{T}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{\theta}=\Upsilon_{\theta^{\prime}}
. This implies realizability due to
\Upsilon_{\pi}=\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}(\mathcal{T}^{\pi})^{T}\Upsilon_{\theta}
under mild conditions.

Additionally, we make several mild assumptions regarding the transition probability
p
and the candidate space
\Theta
, including the sub-Gaussian rewards.
A random variable (vector)
\mathbf{X}
being sub-Gaussian implies its tail probability decaying as fast as Gaussian distribution
, quantified with finite sub-Gaussian norm
\|\mathbf{X}\|_{\psi_{2}}<\infty
.

Assumption 3.2
.

For any
\theta\in\Theta
, the random element
Z(s,a;\theta)
, which follows
\Upsilon_{\theta}(s,a)
, has finite expectation of norms, and the reward distributions conditioned on
s,a
(namely,
R(s,a)
) are sub-Gaussian, i.e.,

\displaystyle\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\sup_{s,a}\mathbb{E}\|Z(s,a;\theta)\|<\infty\mbox{ and }\sup_{s,a}\|R(s,a)\|_{\psi_{2}}<\infty.

We further explain the sub-Gaussian norm
\|\cdot\|_{\psi_{2}}
in Appendix
A.5
. Note that the bounded reward assumptions that are widely adopted (Table
5
of Appendix
D.1
) are stronger than Assumption
3.2
.

Then we can obtain the convergence rate
O(\sqrt{\log(N/\delta)/N})
as follows. Its proof can be found in Appendix
A.7
, and its special case for
\Theta\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{p}
(under Assumption
4.1
) is covered in Corollary
A.4
.

Theorem 3.3
.

(Inaccuracy for realizable scenario)
Under Assumptions
2.1
,
3.1
,
3.2
,
for any
\delta\in(0,1)
, given large enough sample size
N\geq N(\delta)
, our estimator
\hat{\theta}\in\Theta
given by (
13
) satisfies the following bound with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\displaystyle\bar{\mathcal{E}}(\Upsilon_{\hat{\theta}},\Upsilon_{\pi})\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{1}{N}\log\left(\frac{(|\mathcal{S\times A}|+N)}{\delta}\right)},

where
N(\delta)
depends on the complexity of
\Theta
(details in (
43
) Appendix
A.7.5
) and
\lesssim
means smaller than or equal to the given bound (RHS) multiplied by a positive number that does not depend on
N
.

The effects of all other factors (e.g.
d
,
\gamma
, model complexity, sub-Gaussianity, etc.) can be seen from the finite-sample error bound of Appendix
A.7.5
. We also provide the closed-form estimator for the continuous state-action space with deterministic transition in
A.9.1
, along with its theoretical analysis (Theorem
A.7
).

Remark 3.4
.

FLE
(
Wu
et al. 2023
)
also provided error bounds in their Corollary 4.14. However, their inaccuracy measure is for
(S,A)
-marginalized distribution. Theorem
3.3
focuses on expectation-extended inaccuracy, taking into account inaccuracy levels for every state-action pair, which is more informative than that in FLE.

Remark 3.5
.

It is not easy to form a direct theoretical comparison between EBRM and FLE, since our results for EBRM is based on weaker assumptions (e.g., realizability instead of completeness, subgaussianity instead of bounded rewards). However, if we assume bounded 1-dimensional (
d=1
) tabular setting, we can derive a faster guarantee of convergence rate in Wasserstein-
p
metric for all
p\geq 1
for EBRM. See (
48
) of Appendix
A.10
.

4
NON-REALIZABLE SETTINGS

Without abundant data, we normally resort to a simpler model (e.g. linear MDP by
Wang
et al. 2023
), where non-realizability can arise as a serious issue.
Even in traditional RL, theoretical analysis under non-realizability (e.g.,
Perdomo et al. 2023
and
Miyaguchi 2021
) is of significant interest.
In DRL, as our target is to estimate the conditional distribution of return given any state-action pair, which is an infinite-dimensional object, non-realizability could be serious and challenging even for tabular settings, not to say non-tabular settings.
In this section, we investigate non-realizability.

4.1
Combating Non-realizability With Multi-step Extensions

First, we note that, even under non-realizability, Theorem
2.2
still holds. Then, as long as the violation of Assumption
3.1
is minuscule and so
\inf_{\theta\in\Theta}\bar{\mathcal{E}}(\Upsilon_{\theta},\mathcal{T}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{\theta})\approx 0
, we can still derive a tight error bound (shown in Appendix
A.7.6
). However, if the violation is severe (i.e., large extent of non-realizability), the minimal Bellman residual
\inf_{\theta\in\Theta}\bar{\mathcal{E}}(\Upsilon_{\theta},\mathcal{T}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{\theta})
can be large.
This can lead to a possibly serious mismatch between the “best approximation”
\tilde{\theta}
(the target) and the Bellman residual minimizer
\theta_{*}
:

\tilde{\theta}:=\arg\min_{\theta\in\Theta}\bar{\mathcal{E}}(\Upsilon_{\theta},\Upsilon_{\pi})\neq\arg\min_{\theta\in\Theta}\bar{\mathcal{E}}(\Upsilon_{\theta},\mathcal{T}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{\theta})=:\theta_{*}.

(14)

Clearly, this mismatch is not due to sample variability, so it is unrealistic to hope that
\hat{\theta}
defined by (
13
) would necessarily converge in probability to
\tilde{\theta}
as
N\rightarrow\infty
.

To solve this issue, we propose a new approach. Temporarily ignoring mathematical rigor, the most important insight is that we can approximate
(\mathcal{T}^{\pi})^{m}\Upsilon\approx\Upsilon_{\pi}
with sufficiently large step level
m\in\mathbb{N}
.
Thanks to the properties of energy distance,
we have the following for some constant
C>0
:

\displaystyle\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}|\bar{\mathcal{E}}(\Upsilon_{\theta},(\mathcal{T}^{\pi})^{m}\Upsilon_{\theta})-\bar{\mathcal{E}}(\Upsilon_{\theta},\Upsilon_{\pi})|\leq C\gamma^{m},

(15)

as shown in Appendix
C.1.9
.
As
m\rightarrow\infty
, the RHS of (
15
) shrinks to zero, making
m
-step Bellman residual
\bar{\mathcal{E}}(\Upsilon_{\theta},(\mathcal{T}^{\pi})^{m}\Upsilon_{\theta})
approximate the inaccuracy function
\bar{\mathcal{E}}(\Upsilon_{\theta},\Upsilon_{\pi})
. This leads the two minimizers to be close, as illustrated schematically in Figure
2
and exemplified in Appendix
C.2.2
:

\displaystyle\theta_{*}^{(m)}

\displaystyle:=\arg\min_{\theta\in\Theta}\bar{\mathcal{E}}(\Upsilon_{\theta},(\mathcal{T}^{\pi})^{m}\Upsilon_{\theta})

(16)

\displaystyle\approx\arg\min_{\theta\in\Theta}\bar{\mathcal{E}}(\Upsilon_{\theta},\Upsilon_{\pi})=:\tilde{\theta}\ \ \text{for large enough }m.

One can intuitively guess that larger step level
m
is required when the extent of non-realizability is large.

Multi-step has been employed to improve sample efficiency in traditional RL
(
Munos et al. 2016
;
Chen et al. 2021
, e.g.,)
and distributional RL
(
Tang et al. 2022
, e.g.,)
.
However, to the best of our knowledge, it has never been used to address non-realizability, as in (
16
).

4.2
Splitting-based Estimator

Given a value of step level
m\in\mathbb{N}
, our task is to estimate the multi-step Bellman residual
\bar{\mathcal{E}}(\Upsilon_{\theta},(\mathcal{T}^{\pi})^{m}\Upsilon_{\theta})
. One natural way is to use the whole data
\mathcal{D}
to estimate
\hat{\mathcal{T}}^{\pi}
and re-use it for
m
consecutive times
(\hat{\mathcal{T}}^{\pi})^{m}
. However, this causes two issues: (1) This generally requires the computation of
N^{m}
trajectories of
s,a,r^{(1)},s^{(1)},a^{(1)},\cdots r^{(m)},s^{(m)},a^{(m)}
(as discussed in Appendix
B.1
), leading to a heavy computational burden. (2) Re-using the same data
\mathcal{D}
for every step of transition causes dependence between our samples, leading to difficulty in deriving theoretical guarantee.

We introduce a splitting-based estimator in this subsection for the sake of strong theoretical guarantee, but will also explain a more practical estimator based on bootstrap in the following subsection. In the splitting-based method, we divide the data into
m
equally-sized subdatasets
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{D}_{1}\cup\cdots\cup\mathcal{D}_{m}
, and use each subdataset to estimate the Bellman operator at
j
-th step, say
\hat{\mathcal{T}}_{j}^{\pi}
. By estimating
(\mathcal{T}^{\pi})^{m}
via
\hat{\mathcal{T}}_{1:m}^{\pi}:=\hat{\mathcal{T}}_{1}^{\pi}\cdots\hat{\mathcal{T}}_{m}^{\pi}
, we can define the following objective functions:

\displaystyle F_{m}(\theta):=\bar{\mathcal{E}}\big(\Upsilon_{\theta},(\mathcal{T}^{\pi})^{m}\Upsilon_{\theta}\big)\ \ \&\ \ \theta_{*}^{(m)}:=\arg\min_{\theta\in\Theta}F_{m}(\theta)

\displaystyle F(\theta):=\bar{\mathcal{E}}\big(\Upsilon_{\theta},\Upsilon_{\pi}\big)\ \ \&\ \ \tilde{\theta}:=\arg\min_{\theta\in\Theta}F(\theta)

(17)

\displaystyle\hat{F}_{m}(\theta):=\hat{\bar{\mathcal{E}}}(\Upsilon_{\theta},\hat{\mathcal{T}}_{1:m}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{\theta})\ \ \&\ \ \hat{\theta}^{(m)}:=\arg\min_{\theta\in\Theta}\hat{F}_{m}(\theta).

For the theoretical investigation, we make the following two assumptions.

Assumption 4.1
.

The candidate space
\Theta\subset\mathbb{R}^{p}
is compact. There exists
L>0
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{W}_{1,\infty}(\Upsilon_{\theta_{1}},\Upsilon_{\theta_{2}})\leq L\|\theta_{1}-\theta_{2}\|\quad\mbox{for}\quad\forall\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\in\Theta,

where
\mathbb{W}_{1,\infty}
is the supremum-extended (
3
) Wasserstein-1 metric
\mathbb{W}_{1}
.

We can replace
\mathbb{W}_{1,\infty}
of Assumption
4.1
with another metric, as long as it satisfies the properties mentioned in Appendix
B.2
. Lipschitz continuity is involved in bounding the entropy with respect to
\mathbb{W}_{1,\infty}
.
(See Remark
A.5
.)

Remark 4.2
.

Assumption
4.1
is used for bounding the model complexity. Of course, it may be strong for complex models (e.g., deep neural network). However, we expect that such complex models can almost satisfy realizability, so we can apply single-step estimation (
8
).

Assumption 4.3
.

There exists a constant
c>0
such that for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
(or sufficiently large
m
) we have

F_{m}(\theta)\geq F_{m}(\theta_{*}^{(m)})+c\cdot\|\theta-\theta_{*}^{(m)}\|^{2}
for their unique minimizers
\theta_{*}^{(m)}
.

This assumption is imposed to obtain the convergence rate of our estimator by adopting M-estimation theory
(
Van der
Vaart 2000
, Chapter 5)
. The quadratic bound can be relaxed to general
q\geq 1
(e.g. Assumption
B.5
). Albeit not necessary, we shall assume uniqueness of
\theta_{*}^{(m)}
and
\tilde{\theta}
for convenience. Although Assumption
4.3
seems to be strong, we relax this to Assumption
B.5
for the more practical bootstrap estimator (Section
4.3
). Moreover, we anticipate that Assumption
B.5
with
q=2
shall imply Assumption
4.3
under mild conditions due to the uniform convergence of
F_{m}(\cdot)
to
F(\cdot)
shown in (
15
). Although Assumption
B.5
with
q=2
seems similar to a strong convexity assumption, this can accommodate non-convex functions (see Appendix
C.2.3
).
Proof is given in Appendix
B.7
.

Theorem 4.4
.

(Inaccuracy for non-realizable scenario)
Under Assumptions
2.1
,
3.2
,
4.1
,
4.3
, if we set as
m=\lfloor\log_{\frac{1}{\gamma}}(C\cdot N)\rfloor
for an arbitrary
C>0
, then the estimator
\hat{\theta}^{(m)}
(
17
) satisfies the following convergence rate,

\displaystyle\bar{\mathcal{E}}(\Upsilon_{\hat{\theta}^{(m)}},\Upsilon_{\tilde{\theta}})\leq\frac{L\big(L\sqrt{p}+\sqrt{\frac{d}{1-\gamma}}\big)}{\sqrt{p_{\rm min}}}O_{p}\bigg(\sqrt{\frac{(\log_{\frac{1}{\gamma}}N)^{3}}{N}}\bigg).

Under realizability (Assumption
3.1
), where we have
\Upsilon_{\tilde{\theta}}=\Upsilon_{\pi}
, Theorem
4.4
implies convergence towards
\Upsilon_{\pi}
. Its
convergence rate degenerates into that of Theorem
3.3
up to logarithmic difference. Without realizability, we still have convergence towards the best approximation
\tilde{\theta}
(
14
) in the same rate. This multi-step idea can be extended towards non-tabular setting. However, this requires the data to be collected as trajectories, as assumed by
Huang et al. 2022
.

4.3
Bootstrap-based Estimator

Although splitting-based estimator solves the dependence issue, it requires computation of
(N/m)^{m}
many trajectories by the same logic as Appendix
B.1
. This is still very large. Therefore, we propose another estimator that is based on bootstrap, which can be more practical.
The idea of bootstrap-based estimator is to approximate
(\hat{\mathcal{T}}^{\pi})^{m}
by bootstrapping from the
N^{m}
many possible trajectories. Denoting the bootstrap-based estimator as
\mathcal{B}_{m}
and letting
M
be the number of resampled trajectories, we can approximate
(\hat{\mathcal{T}}^{\pi})^{m}
with
\mathcal{B}_{m}
as
M\rightarrow\infty
. Here, we define the objective functions, including re-definition of
\hat{F}_{m}
which is different from that of (
17
). We will refer to this method as EBRM-multi-step. Its exact procedure is summarized in Algorithm
2
of Appendix
B.3
.

\displaystyle\hat{F}_{m}(\theta):=\hat{\bar{\mathcal{E}}}\big(\Upsilon_{\theta},(\hat{\mathcal{T}}^{\pi})^{m}\Upsilon_{\theta}\big)\ \ \&\ \ \hat{\theta}^{(m)}:=\arg\min_{\theta\in\Theta}\hat{F}_{m}(\theta),

\displaystyle\hat{F}_{m}^{(B)}(\theta):=\hat{\bar{\mathcal{E}}}\big(\Upsilon_{\theta},\mathcal{B}_{m}\Upsilon_{\theta}\big)\ \ \&\ \ \hat{\theta}_{m}^{(B)}:=\arg\min_{\theta\in\Theta}\hat{F}_{m}^{(B)}(\theta).

(18)

and

, respectively.

(blue: EBRM, orange: QRDQN, green: MMDQN).

Since we can choose the value of
M
(number of resampled trajectories), this algorithm is indeed more practical than the splitting-based estimator suggested in the previous section. We could also develop a theoretical guarantee for convergence towards the best approximation
\Upsilon_{\tilde{\theta}}
(
17
) that we can achieve under the non-realizability. However, since we cannot be free from the dependence issue, our proof technique ends up with a slower convergence guarantee (Theorem
B.7
of Appendix
B.6.4
), which is in fact based on Assumption
B.5
(weaker than Assumption
4.3
). Its finite-sample error bound is given in Appendix
B.6.2
.

5
EXPERIMENTS

In this section, we conduct a numerical study of the proposed method. We fix a target policy
\pi
and then estimate the target
\Upsilon_{\pi}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathcal{S\times A}}
with offline data. We compared its accuracy with other DRL methods based on Energy distance and Wasserstein metric.
Its main purpose lies in confirming the theoretical advantage of EBRM that we proved in Sections
3
and
4
.
All simulation codes can be found in
https://github.com/hse1223/Distributional-OPE-EBRM.git
.

5.1
OpenAI-gym Games

We applied our method in three OpenAI-gym classical games: acrobot, cartpole, and mountaincar, the second of which was used in comparing various OPE methods in traditional (non-distributional) RL
(
Wang
et al. 2023
;
Narita
et al. 2021
, e.g.,)
. Under the discount rate
\gamma=0.90
, we have formed our model with neural network and estimated the true distributions
Z_{\pi}(s,a)\sim\Upsilon_{\pi}(s,a)
with particles (or Dirac delta’s), which is one of the most intuitive ways of expressing a distribution. For fair comparison, we compared our method EBRM with other particle (or quantile) based methods, QRDQN
(
Dabney
et al. 2018
)
and MMDRL
(
Nguyen-Tang
et al. 2021
)
with the same neural network structure (Appendix
D.2.1
). FLE
(
Wu
et al. 2023
)
is not included, as it requires conditional densities which cannot be directly constructed based on particles.

It is difficult to compute the true expectation-extended distance (
5
) for assessment purposes under these games, due to its large cardinality
|\mathcal{S\times A}|
.
Therefore, we adopted a simpler way of measuring the inaccuracy, as used in Corollary 4.14 of
Wu
et al. 2023
, which is the statistical distance between the marginalized distributions
\Upsilon^{marginal}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, i.e., the mixture of
\{\Upsilon(s,a):s,a\in\mathcal{S\times A}\}
with weights
\{\pi_{\mu}(s,a):s,a\in\mathcal{S\times A}\}
.
Here,
\pi_{\mu}
is combination of
S\sim\mu
and
A\sim\pi(\cdot|A)
. In Table
2
(top), we compare the inaccuracy of the estimated marginal distribution based on both energy distance and Wasserstein-1 metric. Our method EBRM showed the most accurate results in all cases.
We also conducted more experiments based on the Cartpole games by varying the neural network model size and the sample size. Figure
1
shows the superior performance of EBRM for small neural network model.
We see similar comparison results for the large neural network model (see Appendix
D.2.2
).

5.2
Experiments Under Non-completeness

In addition to OpenAI-gym games, we have also performed simulations to demonstrate the theoretical guarantee of EBRM when completeness is not satisfied (Theorems
3.3
and
B.7
). Toward that end, we will compare our bootstrap-based EBRM (
18
) with a method that is based on the completeness assumption. We assumed a simple tabular setting with
\mathcal{S}=\{1,2,\dots,30\}
and
\mathcal{A}=\{-1,1\}
(details in Appendix
D.3.1
).
A practical algorithm for choosing the step level is explained in Appendix
D.4.1
.

We have tried both realizable settings (big and small variances) and non-realizable settings (big and small
\gamma
), whose details are explained in Appendices
D.3.1
–
D.3.3
. EBRM outperformed its competitors in most settings (Tables
19
–
24
). In particular, when completeness is severely violated (non-realizable and
\gamma=0.99
in Table
2
(bottom)), EBRM successfully approximated the minimum inaccuracy
\min_{\theta\in\Theta}\bar{\mathcal{E}}(\Upsilon_{\theta},\Upsilon_{\pi})\approx 63.216
. The worse performance of FLE is not surprising, since it requires the completeness assumption (details in Appendix
D.7
).
Besides, we have conducted an ablation study for the step level of EBRM, and
demonstrated that the step level plays a key role in combating non-realizability.
See Appendix
D.6
for more details.

Appendix A
PROOFS FOR SECTIONS
2
AND
3

A.1
Bounding Radon-Nikodym Derivative

Define
q_{b_{\mu}}^{\pi:t}
to be the density of the probability of
S^{(t)},A^{(t)}
where
A^{(j)}\sim\pi(\cdot|S^{(j)})
and
(R^{(j)},S^{(j)})\sim p(\cdots|S^{(j-1)},A^{(j-1)})
for
1\leq j\leq t
and
(S^{(0)},A^{(0)})\sim b_{\mu}
. Let
t\in\mathbb{N}
be arbitrary. Then we have the following for all
s,a\in\mathcal{S\times A}
by Assumption
2.1
, with
\nu
being the underlying measure of
\mathcal{S\times A}
,

\displaystyle\frac{q_{b_{\mu}}^{\pi:t}(s,a)}{b_{\mu}(s,a)}\leq\int_{\mathcal{S\times A}}q^{\pi}(s,a|\tilde{s},\tilde{a})\cdot q_{b_{\mu}}^{\pi:t-1}(\tilde{s},\tilde{a})\mathrm{d}\nu(\tilde{s},\tilde{a})\cdot\frac{1}{b_{\mu}(s,a)}\leq\sup_{\tilde{s},\tilde{a}}\sup_{s,a}\frac{q^{\pi}(s,a|\tilde{s},\tilde{a})}{b_{\mu}(s,a)}<\infty,

where
q_{b_{\mu}}^{\pi:0}=b_{\mu}
. Since
t\in\mathbb{N}
was arbitrary, this implies existence of
C(t),C_{\rm sup}\in(0,\infty)
such that

\displaystyle\sup_{s,a}\bigg\{\frac{q_{b_{\mu}}^{\pi:t}(s,a)}{b_{\mu}(s,a)}\bigg\}\leq C(t)\leq C_{{\rm sup}}<\infty\ \ \text{for }\forall\ t\in\mathbb{N}.

(19)

A.2
Proof Of Theorem
2.2

A.2.1
Properties Of Distance

Property 1
.

\eta
satisfies translation-invariance and scale-sensitivity of order
\beta_{0}>0
. That is, with
z
being an arbitrary (nonrandom) constant of computable size and
c\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle\eta\{\mathcal{L}(z+X),\mathcal{L}(z+Y)\}\leq\eta\{\mathcal{L}(X),\mathcal{L}(Y)\}\ \ \&\ \ \eta\{\mathcal{L}(cX),\mathcal{L}(cY)\}\leq|c|^{\beta_{0}}\eta\{\mathcal{L}(X),\mathcal{L}(Y)\}.

Property 2
.

Letting
\mu_{1}(\cdot),\mu_{2}(\cdot):\mathcal{Z}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
have different probability measures depending on the index random variable
Z\in\mathcal{Z}
that follows a distribution
P(\cdot)
, the distance between probability-mixtures
\int_{\mathcal{Z}}\mu_{1}(z)\mathrm{d}P(z)
and
\int_{\mathcal{Z}}\mu_{2}(z)\mathrm{d}P(z)
satisfies convexity, that is

\displaystyle\eta\bigg\{\int_{\mathcal{Z}}\mu_{1}(z)\mathrm{d}P(z),\int_{\mathcal{Z}}\mu_{2}(z)\mathrm{d}P(z)\bigg\}\leq\int_{\mathcal{Z}}\eta\{\mu_{1}(z),\mu_{2}(z)\}\mathrm{d}P(z)=:\mathbb{E}_{Z\sim P}[\eta\{\mu_{1}(Z),\mu_{2}(Z)\}].

Property 3
.

There exists some value
q\geq 0
such that following relaxed triangular inequality holds for all integers
K\geq 2
,

\displaystyle\eta(\mathcal{L}(X_{0}),\mathcal{L}(X_{K}))

\displaystyle\leq K^{q}\sum_{i=0}^{K-1}\eta(\mathcal{L}(X_{i}),\mathcal{L}(X_{i+1})).

(20)

This is satisfied by all
(q+1)
-powered metric (with nonnegative integer
q\geq 0
), that is
\eta(P,Q)=\rho^{q+1}(P,Q)
for some probability metric
\rho
. Using convexity of
f(x)=x^{q+1}
(
x\geq 0
), we can derive
(x_{1}+\cdots x_{K})^{q+1}\leq K^{q}\cdot(x_{1}^{q+1}+\cdots+x_{K}^{q+1})
, which directly implies that
\eta=\rho^{q+1}
satisfies (
20
).

A.2.2
Proof

Let
\Upsilon\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathcal{S\times A}}
be arbitrarily chosen. Starting with Relaxed Triangular Inequality (
20
) with
K=2
, we obtain the following for an arbitrary
t\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle\eta\bigg\{\Upsilon(s,a),\Upsilon_{\pi}(s,a)\bigg\}

\displaystyle\leq 2^{q}\bigg[\underbrace{\eta\bigg\{\Upsilon(s,a),(\mathcal{T}^{\pi})^{t}\Upsilon(s,a)\bigg\}}_{(a)}+\underbrace{\eta\bigg\{(\mathcal{T}^{\pi})^{t}\Upsilon(s,a),\Upsilon_{\pi}(s,a)\bigg\}}_{(b)}\bigg].

Let us first deal with
(b)
. Define
P_{t}^{\pi}(\cdots|s,a)
to be the probability measure of
(\sum_{i=1}^{t}\gamma^{i-1}R^{(i)},S^{(t)},A^{(t)})
after
t
steps starting from the initial state-action pair
(S,A)=(s,a)
under the given transition probability
(R^{(t)},S^{(t)})\sim p(\cdots|S^{(t-1)},A^{(t-1)})
and the target policy
A^{(t)}\sim\pi(\cdot|S^{(t)})
. Further denoting the probability measure of
y+\gamma^{t}Z(s^{(t)},a^{(t)})
with
Z(s,a)\sim\Upsilon(s,a)
as
(g_{y,\gamma^{t}})_{\#}\Upsilon(s^{(t)},a^{(t)})
for the fixed value of
s^{(t)},a^{(t)}
,
y=\sum_{i=1}^{t}\gamma^{i-1}r^{(i)}
(which aligns with the notation
(g_{r,\gamma})_{\#}
in (
1
)), we can obtain the following by Properties
1
and
2
,

\displaystyle(b)

\displaystyle=\eta\bigg\{(\mathcal{T}^{\pi})^{t}\Upsilon(s,a),(\mathcal{T}^{\pi})^{t}\Upsilon_{\pi}(s,a)\bigg\}\ \ \because(\mathcal{T}^{\pi})^{t}\Upsilon_{\pi}(s,a)=\Upsilon_{\pi}(s,a)

\displaystyle=\eta\bigg\{\int(g_{y,\gamma^{t}})_{\#}\Upsilon(s^{(t)},a^{(t)})\mathrm{d}P_{t}^{\pi}(y,s^{(t)},a^{(t)}|s,a),\int(g_{y,\gamma^{t}})_{\#}\Upsilon_{\pi}(s^{(t)},a^{(t)})\mathrm{d}P_{t}^{\pi}(y,s^{(t)},a^{(t)}|s,a)\bigg\}

\displaystyle\leq\gamma^{t\beta_{0}}\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\bigg[\eta\bigg\{\Upsilon(S^{(t)},A^{(t)}),\Upsilon_{\pi}(S^{(t)},A^{(t)})\bigg\}\bigg|S=s,A=a\bigg].

From now on, we will use
\mathbb{E}_{b_{\mu}}
and
\mathbb{E}_{q_{b_{\mu}}^{\pi:t}}
to denote the expectation with respect to the probability
(S,A)\sim b_{\mu}
and
(S,A)\sim q_{b_{\mu}}^{\pi:t}
. Treating
(S,A)
as random, we can obtain the following,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{b_{\mu}}\bigg[(b)\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq\gamma^{t\beta_{0}}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\eta\bigg\{\Upsilon(S^{(t)},A^{(t)}),\Upsilon_{\pi}(S^{(t)},A^{(t)}))\bigg\}\bigg|(S,A)\sim b_{\mu},\ A^{(i)}\sim\pi(\cdot|S^{(i)})\ \forall i\geq 1\bigg]

\displaystyle=\gamma^{t\beta_{0}}\mathbb{E}_{q_{b_{\mu}}^{\pi:t}}\bigg[\eta\bigg\{\Upsilon(S,A),\Upsilon_{\pi}(S,A)\bigg\}\bigg]\leq C(t)\cdot\gamma^{t\beta_{0}}\cdot\bar{\eta}(\Upsilon,\Upsilon_{\pi})\ \ \text{by \eqref{Ct_and_Csup}}.

Now let us deal with
(a)
using Property
3
. Let
\Upsilon_{k}(s,a)=(\mathcal{T}^{\pi})^{k}\Upsilon(s,a)
. For sufficiently large
t\in\mathbb{N}
, we can bound
\eta(\Upsilon(s,a),(\mathcal{T}^{\pi})^{t}\Upsilon(s,a))
with following by relaxed triangle inequality (
20
) with
K=2
,

\displaystyle 2^{q}\cdot\eta(\Upsilon_{0}(s,a),\Upsilon_{1}(s,a))+(2^{q})^{2}\cdot\eta(\Upsilon_{1}(s,a),\Upsilon_{3}(s,a))+(2^{q})^{3}\cdot\eta(\Upsilon_{3}(s,a),\Upsilon_{7}(s,a))+\cdots

Further applying relaxed triangle inequality (
20
) with general
K\geq 2
to each term with
\eta
, we can formalize it into

\displaystyle(a)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}(2^{q})^{2k-1}\cdot\sum_{j=0}^{s_{1}(k)}\eta\bigg(\Upsilon_{s_{1}(k)+j}(s,a),\Upsilon_{s_{1}(k)+j+1}(s,a)\bigg)\ \ \text{where }s_{1}(k)=2^{k-1}-1.

Therefore we can further obtain following using similar logic as bounding
(b)
with
C_{\rm sup}
defined in (
19
),

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{b_{\mu}}\bigg[(a)\bigg]\leq\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}(2^{q})^{2k-1}\cdot\sum_{j=0}^{s_{1}(k)}\mathbb{E}_{b_{\mu}}\bigg[\eta\bigg\{\Upsilon_{s_{1}(k)+j}(S,A),\Upsilon_{s_{1}(k)+j+1}(S,A)\bigg\}\bigg]\leq C_{{\rm sup}}\cdot B(\gamma;\beta_{0},q)\cdot\bar{\eta}(\Upsilon,\mathcal{T}^{\pi}\Upsilon)

where we have

\displaystyle B(\gamma;\beta_{0},q):=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}(2^{q})^{2k-1}\cdot\sum_{j=0}^{2^{k-1}-1}\gamma^{(2^{k-1}-1+j)\beta_{0}}=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}(2^{q})^{2k-1}\cdot\gamma^{(2^{k-1}-1)\beta_{0}}\cdot\sum_{j=0}^{2^{k-1}-1}(\gamma^{\beta_{0}})^{j}

\displaystyle\leq\bigg(\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}(2^{q})^{2k-1}\cdot\gamma^{(2^{k-1}-1)\beta_{0}}\bigg)\cdot\bigg(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}(\gamma^{\beta_{0}})^{j}\bigg)\leq\frac{1}{2^{q}(1-\gamma^{\beta_{0}})}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}(4^{qk}\cdot\gamma^{(2^{k-1}-1)\beta_{0}})<\infty.

Then, we finally obtain the first result for all
t\in\mathbb{N}
, and the second result by letting
t\rightarrow\infty
,

\displaystyle\bar{\eta}(\Upsilon,\Upsilon_{\pi})\leq 2^{q}C_{{\rm sup}}\cdot\bigg\{B(\gamma;\beta_{0},q)\cdot\bar{\eta}(\Upsilon,\mathcal{T}^{\pi}\Upsilon)+\gamma^{t\beta_{0}}\bar{\eta}(\Upsilon,\Upsilon_{\pi})\bigg\},

\displaystyle\therefore\ \bar{\eta}(\Upsilon,\Upsilon_{\pi})\leq C_{{\rm sup}}B_{1}(\gamma;\beta_{0},q)\cdot\bar{\eta}(\Upsilon,\mathcal{T}^{\pi}\Upsilon)\quad\text{where}\quad B_{1}(\gamma;\beta_{0},q):=\frac{1}{(1-\gamma^{\beta_{0}})}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}(4^{qk}\cdot\gamma^{(2^{k-1}-1)\beta_{0}}).

(21)

A.2.3
Asymptotic Bound With The Discount Rate

One may wonder how large the term
B_{1}(\gamma;\beta_{0},q)
is, with respect to the discount rate
\gamma\in(0,1)
becomes larger, that is
\gamma\rightarrow 1
. For regular metric that satisfies
q=0
(refer to
A.2.1
), it is trivial to derive

\displaystyle B_{1}(\gamma;\beta_{0},q)\leq\bigg(\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{\beta_{0}}}\bigg)^{2}\quad\text{for}\quad q=0.

Therefore, let us assume
q>0
. Then we have

\displaystyle B_{1}(\gamma;\beta_{0},q)=\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{\beta_{0}}}\cdot\bigg\{\sum_{k=1}^{K_{0}}4^{qk}\cdot(\gamma^{\beta_{0}})^{2^{k}}+\sum_{k=K_{0}+1}^{\infty}4^{qk}\cdot(\gamma^{\beta_{0}})^{2^{k}}\bigg\},

where
K_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
is sufficiently large so that
k\geq K_{0}
implies

\displaystyle 4^{qk}\cdot(\gamma^{\beta_{0}})^{2^{k}}\leq(\gamma^{\beta_{0}})^{k},\quad\text{or equivalently,}\quad k\geq\log_{2}k+\log_{2}\log_{\frac{1}{\gamma^{\beta_{0}}}}\bigg(\frac{4^{q}}{\gamma^{\beta_{0}}}\bigg).

Let
c_{0}\in(0,1)
be the maximum value that satisfies
(1-c_{0})x\geq\log_{2}x
for all
x>0
. Numerical experiments tell us
c_{0}\approx 0.47
. Then, it suffices to define
K_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{let}}\lfloor\frac{1}{c_{0}}\cdot\log_{2}\log_{\frac{1}{\gamma^{\beta_{0}}}}(2\cdot 4^{q})\rfloor
.
Then, letting
C(q)=4^{q}/(4^{q}-1)
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{K_{0}}4^{qk}\cdot(\gamma^{\beta_{0}})^{2^{k}}\leq\frac{4^{q}}{4^{q}-1}\cdot(4^{qK_{0}}-1)\leq C(q)\cdot\bigg\{\log_{\frac{1}{\gamma^{\beta_{0}}}}(2\cdot 4^{q})\bigg\}^{\frac{2q}{c_{0}}},

\displaystyle\sum_{k=K_{0}+1}^{\infty}4^{qk}\cdot(\gamma^{\beta_{0}})^{2^{k}}\leq\sum_{k=K_{0}+1}^{\infty}(\gamma^{\beta_{0}})^{k}\leq\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{\beta_{0}}}.

Assuming increasing discount rate
\gamma\rightarrow 1
, by letting
x=\log(1/\gamma^{\beta_{0}})
, we can apply l’Hospital’s rule to obtain
\lim_{\gamma\rightarrow 1}\log_{1/\gamma^{\beta_{0}}}(2\cdot 4^{q})\cdot(1-\gamma^{\beta_{0}})=\log(2\cdot 4^{q})
.
Thus, we eventually obtain the following with the value of
\beta_{0}
and
q
depending on
\eta
(Section
A.2.1
),

\displaystyle B_{1}(\gamma;\beta_{0},q)\lesssim\bigg(\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{\beta_{0}}}\bigg)^{\frac{2q}{c_{0}}+1}\quad\text{as}\quad\gamma\rightarrow 1\qquad\text{with }c_{0}\approx 0.47.

(22)

A.3
Examples Of Valid Probability Distances

Here are examples of probability distances that satisfy properties
A.2.1
, which are of course not necessarily metrics, but mostly are powered metrics. Given probability measures
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}
,
J(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
indicate the class of joint distributions of two marginals
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
.
\mathbf{X},\mathbf{X}^{\prime}\sim\mu_{1}
and
\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}^{\prime}\sim\mu_{2}
are all independent.
F_{\mu}
is cdf of
\mu
.

\displaystyle\mathbb{W}_{p}^{k}\ \text{ for }k\geq p,

\displaystyle\ \text{ where }\mathbb{W}_{p}^{p}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}):=\inf_{J(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\mu_{1},Y\sim\mu_{2}}\|\mathbf{X}-\mathbf{Y}\|^{p},

\displaystyle{\rm MMD}_{\kappa}^{k}\ (0<\kappa<2)\ \text{ for }k\geq 1,

\displaystyle\ \text{ where }{\rm MMD}_{\kappa}^{2}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}):=2\mathbb{E}\{\|\mathbf{X}-\mathbf{Y}\|\}^{\kappa}-\mathbb{E}\{\|\mathbf{X}-\mathbf{X}^{\prime}\|\}^{\kappa}-\mathbb{E}\{\|\mathbf{Y}-\mathbf{Y}^{\prime}\|\}^{\kappa},

\displaystyle l_{p}^{k}\ \text{ for }k\geq 1,

\displaystyle\ \text{ where }l_{p}^{p}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}):=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\big|F_{\mu_{1}}(z)-F_{\mu_{2}}(z)\big|^{p}\mathrm{d}z\quad(d=1).

(23)

Note that energy distance that we used in (
7
) is a special case of
{\rm MMD}_{\kappa}^{k}
with
k=2
and
\kappa=1
.

First, let us justify powered Wasserstein metric. Property
1
can be verified from Section 3.2 of
Bellemare
et al. 2017
. Property
3
holds, since this is a powered metric. As we mentioned beneath (
20
), we have
q=p-1
. Property
2
can be verified by Kantorovich duality mentioned in Lemma C.5 of
Wu
et al. 2023
. Denoting Wasserstein-p metric as
\mathbb{W}_{p}
and letting
\Gamma:=\{(\psi,\phi):\psi,\phi:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R},\ \psi(\mathbf{x})-\phi(\mathbf{y})\leq\|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}\|^{p}\}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{W}_{p}^{p}\bigg\{\int\mu(z)\mathrm{d}P(z),\int\nu(z)\mathrm{d}P(z)\bigg\}=\sup_{\psi,\phi\in\Gamma}\bigg\{\mathbb{E}_{Y\sim\int\mu(z)\mathrm{d}P(z)}\psi(Y)-\mathbb{E}_{Y\sim\int\nu(z)\mathrm{d}P(z)}\phi(Y)\bigg\}

\displaystyle=\sup_{\psi,\phi\in\Gamma}\mathbb{E}_{Z\sim P}\bigg\{\mathbb{E}_{Y\sim\mu(Z)}\psi(Y)-\mathbb{E}_{Y\sim\nu(Z)}\phi(Y)\bigg\}\leq\mathbb{E}_{Z\sim P}\mathbb{W}_{p}^{p}\big\{\mu(Z),\nu(Z)\big\}.

Second, let us justify
{\rm MMD}_{\kappa}^{k}
. Property
1
holds with
\beta_{0}=\kappa/2
. It is straightforward to show, by using the definition (
23
). Property
3
holds with
q=0
, since
{\rm MMD}_{\kappa}
is a metric. Property
2
can be shown by using the fact that maximum mean discrepancy is in fact a norm between two mean embedding in RKHS
(\mathcal{H}_{\kappa},\|\cdot\|_{\kappa})

(
Gretton et al. 2012
)
with kernel
k(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\|\mathbf{x}\|^{\kappa}+\|\mathbf{y}\|^{\kappa}-\|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}\|^{\kappa}
. Denote the mean embedding of
X\sim\mu_{1}(z)
as
m_{1}(z)
and
Y\sim\mu_{2}(z)
as
m_{2}(z)
, we can derive the following by using the convexity of norm,

\displaystyle{\rm MMD}_{\kappa}\bigg\{\int\mu_{1}(z)\mathrm{d}P(z),\int\mu_{2}(z)\mathrm{d}P(z)\bigg\}=\bigg\|\int m_{1}(z)\mathrm{d}P(z)-\int m_{2}(z)\mathrm{d}P(z)\bigg\|_{\kappa}\leq\int\|m_{1}(z)-m_{2}(z)\|_{\kappa}\mathrm{d}P(z)

\displaystyle=\int{\rm MMD}_{\kappa}\big\{\mu_{1}(z),\mu_{2}(z)\big\}\mathrm{d}P(z).

Based on these properties, it is straightforward to see that
\mathcal{E}
that we used in the main text satisfies all the properties with
\beta_{0}=1
and
q=1
. Note that plugging in
K=2
gives us the following special case,

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}\{\mathcal{L}(X_{0}),\mathcal{L}(X_{2})\}\leq 2\cdot[\mathcal{E}\{\mathcal{L}(X_{0}),\mathcal{L}(X_{1})\}+\mathcal{E}\{\mathcal{L}(X_{1}),\mathcal{L}(X_{2})\}].

(24)

Third, let us justify powered norm of cdf. Property
1
is shown by Proposition 3.2 of
Odin and
Charpentier 2020
with
\beta_{0}=1/p
. Property
2
can be shown in the same way with MMD, since it is based on a functional norm,
Property
3
holds with
q=p-1
.

Finally, we can extend this into higher degree. Given that a powered metric
\eta=m^{p}
(with
m
being a probability metric) satisfies properties in
A.2.1
with
\beta_{0}
and
q=p-1
, we can also show that
m^{k}
with
k\geq p
is also a valid distance that satisfies the properties. Property
1
can be shown to hold, by replacing
\beta_{0}
with
\beta_{0}^{k/p}
. Property
3
holds with
q=k-1
. Property
2
can be shown as follows,

\displaystyle m^{k}\bigg(\int\mu_{1}(z)\mathrm{d}P(z),\int\mu_{2}(z)\mathrm{d}P(z)\bigg)=\bigg\{m^{p}\bigg(\int\mu_{1}(z)\mathrm{d}P(z),\int\mu_{2}(z)\mathrm{d}P(z)\bigg)\bigg\}^{k/p}

\displaystyle\leq\bigg(\mathbb{E}_{Z\sim P}\big\{m^{p}(\mu_{1}(Z),\mu_{2}(Z))\big\}\bigg)^{k/p}\leq\mathbb{E}_{Z\sim P}\big\{m^{k}(\mu_{1}(Z),\mu_{2}(Z))\big\}.

A.4
Algorithm Of Single-step EBRM

Here is the algorithm for single-step EBRM (
12
).

Algorithm 1

EBRM-single-step

Input:

\Theta
,
\mathcal{D}=\{(s_{i},a_{i},r_{i},s_{i}^{\prime})\}_{i=1}^{N}

Output:

\hat{\theta}

Estimate
\hat{b}_{\mu}
and
\hat{p}
.
\triangleright
Refer to Equation (
9
).

Compute
\hat{\theta}=\arg\min_{\theta\in\Theta}\hat{\bar{\mathcal{E}}}(\Upsilon_{\theta},\hat{\mathcal{T}}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{\theta})
.
\triangleright
Refer to Equations (
11
) and (
12
).

A.5
Explanation Of Sub-Gaussian Norm

Sub-Gaussianity can be quantified with sub-Gaussian norm
\|\cdot\|_{\psi_{2}}:\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\ \text{or }\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
(Definitions 2.5.6 and 3.4.1 by
Vershynin 2018
. The left and right are for random variable and random vector, respectively.

\displaystyle\|X\|_{\psi_{2}}:=\inf\bigg\{t>0:\ \mathbb{E}\big\{\exp(X^{2}/t^{2})\big\}\leq 2\bigg\}\ (d=1)\quad\&\quad\|\mathbf{X}\|_{\psi_{2}}:=\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\|\mathbf{x}\|=1}\|\langle\mathbf{X},\mathbf{x}\rangle\|_{\psi_{2}}.

(25)

Sub-Gaussian norm is verified to be a valid norm in Exercise 2.5.7 suggested by
Vershynin 2018
. Random variable (vector)
\mathbf{X}
is called sub-Gaussian if it satisfies
\|\mathbf{X}\|_{\psi_{2}}<\infty
. A lot of useful inequalilties, such as Dudley’s integral inequality and and Hoeffding’s inequality (Theorems 8.1.6, 2.6.2 of
Vershynin 2018
) are based on sub-Gaussianity assumption.

A.6
Bounding Expectation-difference With Wasserstein-1 Metric

Let
c\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}\in[0,1]
, and
(s,a),(\tilde{s},\tilde{a})\in\mathcal{S\times A}
be arbitrary,
Z_{i}(s,a)\sim\Upsilon_{i}(s,a)
with
Z_{1}(s,a)
,
Z_{2}(\tilde{s},\tilde{a})
and
Z_{3}(s,a)
,
Z_{4}(\tilde{s},\tilde{a})
being pairwise independent. Letting
J_{13}
be the possible dependence structures (or joint distributions) between marginal distributions of
Z_{1}(s,a)
and
Z_{2}(s,a)
, and
J_{24}
be that between
Z_{3}(\tilde{s},\tilde{a})
and
Z_{4}(\tilde{s},\tilde{a})
, we have

\displaystyle\bigg|\mathbb{E}\|c+\gamma_{1}

\displaystyle Z_{1}(s,a)-\gamma_{2}Z_{2}(\tilde{s},\tilde{a})\|-\mathbb{E}\|c+\gamma_{1}Z_{3}(s,a)-\gamma_{2}Z_{4}(\tilde{s},\tilde{a})\|\bigg|

\displaystyle=\inf_{J_{13},J_{24}}\bigg|\mathbb{E}\|c+\gamma_{1}Z_{1}(s,a)-\gamma_{2}Z_{2}(\tilde{s},\tilde{a})\|-\mathbb{E}\|c+\gamma_{1}Z_{3}(s,a)-\gamma_{2}Z_{4}(\tilde{s},\tilde{a})\|\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\inf_{J_{13},J_{24}}\mathbb{E}\|c+\gamma_{1}Z_{1}(s,a)-\gamma_{2}Z_{2}(\tilde{s},\tilde{a})-c-\gamma_{1}Z_{3}(s,a)+\gamma_{2}Z_{4}(\tilde{s},\tilde{a})\|

\displaystyle=\inf_{J_{13}}\mathbb{E}\|\gamma_{1}Z_{1}(s,a)-\gamma_{1}Z_{3}(s,a)\|+\inf_{J_{24}}\mathbb{E}\|\gamma_{2}Z_{2}(\tilde{s},\tilde{a})-\gamma_{2}Z_{4}(\tilde{s},\tilde{a})\|

\displaystyle\leq\gamma_{1}\cdot\mathbb{W}_{1,\infty}(\Upsilon_{1},\Upsilon_{3})+\gamma_{2}\cdot\mathbb{W}_{1,\infty}(\Upsilon_{2},\Upsilon_{4}),

where the second last line holds by the definition of Wasserstein-1 metric.

A.7
Proof Of Theorem
3.3

Throughout the proof, we will use
C>0
,
C_{k}>0
(
k\in\mathbb{N}
) to denote appropriate universal constants.

In addition, we define the minimizer of Bellman residual
\theta_{*}:\in\arg\min_{\theta\in\Theta}\bar{\mathcal{E}}(\Upsilon_{\theta},\mathcal{T}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{\theta})
.
Since
\Upsilon_{\pi}=\Upsilon_{\theta^{\prime}}
for some
\theta^{\prime}\in\Theta
by Assumption
3.1
, we have
\bar{\mathcal{E}}(\Upsilon_{\theta^{\prime}},\mathcal{T}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{\theta^{\prime}})=\bar{\mathcal{E}}(\Upsilon_{\pi},\mathcal{T}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{\pi})=0
, thereby becoming the minimizer of Bellman residual. Then we can let
\theta_{*}=\theta^{\prime}
, and have
\bar{\mathcal{E}}(\Upsilon_{\theta_{*}},\mathcal{T}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{\theta_{*}})=0
, that is
\Upsilon_{\theta_{*}}=\mathcal{T}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{\theta_{*}}
.

Lastly, we would like to allow abuse of notation
\mathbb{W}_{1,\infty}(\theta_{1},\theta_{2}):=\mathbb{W}_{1,\infty}(\Upsilon_{\theta_{1}},\Upsilon_{\theta_{2}})
, with which we will define the diameter
{\rm diam}(\Theta;\mathbb{W}_{1,\infty}):=\sup_{\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\in\Theta}\{\mathbb{W}_{1,\infty}(\theta_{1},\theta_{2})\}
. Based on the metric
\mathbb{W}_{1,\infty}
, we will quantify the model complexity with
covering number
(Definition 4.2.2 by
Vershynin 2018
). With
N_{\mathbb{W}_{1,\infty}}(\theta,t):=\{\theta^{\prime}\in\Theta:\mathbb{W}_{1,\infty}(\theta^{\prime},\theta)<t\}
being
t
-neighborhood of
\theta\in\Theta
, we define the covering number as
\mathcal{N}(\Theta,\mathbb{W}_{1,\infty},t):=\min\{\tilde{M}\in\mathbb{N}:\exists\theta_{1},\cdots,\theta_{\tilde{M}}\ \text{s.t. }\Theta\subset\cup_{i=1}^{\tilde{M}}N_{\mathbb{W}_{1,\infty}}(\theta_{i},t)\}
.

However, note that
\mathbb{W}_{1,\infty}
in the previous paragraph can be replaced with a general distance measure
\tilde{\eta}
, as long as it satisfies the first two properties suggested in Appendix
B.2
.

A.7.1
Decomposition Into Two Discrepancies

Defining
\Gamma_{N}
and
\Delta_{N}
as

\displaystyle\Gamma_{N}:=\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\bar{\mathcal{E}}(\mathcal{T}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{\theta},\hat{\mathcal{T}}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{\theta})\ \ \&\ \ \Delta_{N}:=\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\bigg|\bar{\mathcal{E}}(\Upsilon_{\theta},\hat{\mathcal{T}}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{\theta})-\hat{\bar{\mathcal{E}}}(\Upsilon_{\theta},\hat{\mathcal{T}}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{\theta})\bigg|,

we can decompose the term
\bar{\mathcal{E}}(\Upsilon_{\hat{\theta}},\mathcal{T}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{\hat{\theta}})
as follows.

\displaystyle\bar{\mathcal{E}}(\Upsilon_{\hat{\theta}},\mathcal{T}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{\hat{\theta}})

\displaystyle\leq 2\cdot\bigg\{\bar{\mathcal{E}}(\Upsilon_{\hat{\theta}},\hat{\mathcal{T}}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{\hat{\theta}})+\bar{\mathcal{E}}(\hat{\mathcal{T}}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{\hat{\theta}},\mathcal{T}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{\hat{\theta}})\bigg\}

\displaystyle\leq 2\cdot\bigg\{\hat{\bar{\mathcal{E}}}(\Upsilon_{\hat{\theta}},\hat{\mathcal{T}}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{\hat{\theta}})+\bigg|\hat{\bar{\mathcal{E}}}(\Upsilon_{\hat{\theta}},\hat{\mathcal{T}}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{\hat{\theta}})-\bar{\mathcal{E}}(\Upsilon_{\hat{\theta}},\hat{\mathcal{T}}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{\hat{\theta}})\bigg|+\bar{\mathcal{E}}(\hat{\mathcal{T}}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{\hat{\theta}},\mathcal{T}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{\hat{\theta}})\bigg\}

\displaystyle\leq 2\cdot\bigg\{\hat{\bar{\mathcal{E}}}(\Upsilon_{\theta_{*}},\hat{\mathcal{T}}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{\theta_{*}})+\Delta_{N}+\Gamma_{N}\bigg\}

\displaystyle\leq 2\cdot\bigg\{\bar{\mathcal{E}}(\Upsilon_{\theta_{*}},\hat{\mathcal{T}}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{\theta_{*}})+\bigg|\hat{\bar{\mathcal{E}}}(\Upsilon_{\theta_{*}},\hat{\mathcal{T}}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{\theta_{*}})-\bar{\mathcal{E}}(\Upsilon_{\theta_{*}},\hat{\mathcal{T}}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{\theta_{*}})\bigg|+\Delta_{N}+\Gamma_{N}\bigg\}

(26)

\displaystyle\leq 2\cdot\bigg\{\bar{\mathcal{E}}(\mathcal{T}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{\theta_{*}},\hat{\mathcal{T}}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{\theta_{*}})+2\Gamma_{N}+\Delta_{N}\bigg\}\qquad\text{ by Assumption \ref{realizable}}

\displaystyle\leq 4\cdot(\Gamma_{N}+\Delta_{N}).

Combined with the result (
21
) of Theorem
2.2
that requires Assumption
2.1
, it leads to the following bound,

\displaystyle\bar{\mathcal{E}}(\Upsilon_{\hat{\theta}},\Upsilon_{\pi})\leq 8C_{{\rm sup}}B_{1}(\gamma)\cdot(\Gamma_{N}+\Delta_{N}),\quad\mbox{where}

(27)

\displaystyle B_{1}(\gamma):=B_{1}(\gamma;1,1)=\frac{1}{2(1-\gamma)}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}4^{k}\gamma^{2^{k-1}-1}\quad\mbox{by definition above \eqref{Fundamental_Realizable_specific}}

since we have verified
\beta_{0}=1
in
A.3
. Now it suffices to bound
\Gamma_{N}
and
\Delta_{N}
, which will be referred to as
Bellman discrepancy
and
state-action discrepancy
to indicate the sources of error,
\mathcal{T}^{\pi}
and
b_{\mu}(s,a)
, respectively. Before we proceed, we list several properties of sub-Gaussian norm (
25
) that we will utilize in our analysis. Corresponding proofs can be found in Section
C.1.2
.

Remark A.1
.

(Properties of sub-Gaussian norm) We have the following properties regarding sub-Gaussian norm,

1.

For
X\sim Ber(p)
, we have
\|X\|_{\psi_{2}}\leq 1/\sqrt{\log 2}
.

2.

For a constant
c\in\mathbb{R}
,
\|c\|_{\psi_{2}}=c/\sqrt{\log 2}
.

3.

For a random variable
X\in\mathbb{R}
,
\|\mathbb{E}(X)\|_{\psi_{2}}\leq\|X\|_{\psi_{2}}
holds.

4.

For a random vector
\mathbf{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\big\|\|\mathbf{X}\|\big\|_{\psi_{2}}\leq d\big\|\mathbf{X}\big\|_{\psi_{2}}
holds.

5.

For a random variable
X\in\mathbb{R}
,
\|X-\mathbb{E}(X)\|_{\psi_{2}}\leq C\|X\|_{\psi_{2}}
holds.

6.

For iid mean-zero random variables
X_{1},\cdots,X_{n}
, we have
\|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}\|_{\psi_{2}}\leq\frac{C}{\sqrt{n}}\cdot\|X_{1}\|_{\psi_{2}}.

A.7.2
Conditioning On Sufficient Sample Size For Each State-action Pair

Prior to bounding
\Gamma_{N}
and
\Delta_{N}
of (
27
), we will first condition upon an event where each state-action pair is observed
sufficiently many times
.

Before we proceed, we should note that the given probability space
(\Omega,\Sigma,\mathbb{P})
can be factorized into two stages. Letting
\mathbf{N}=(N(s,a))_{s,a\in\mathcal{S\times A}}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{S\times A}}
to be a random vector that indicates the observed number of samples for each state-action pair, we can see that
(\Omega,\Sigma,\mathbb{P})
consists of two consecutive probability events denoted as follows,

Stage 1:

\displaystyle\ (\Omega_{\mathcal{S\times A}},\Sigma_{\mathcal{S\times A}},\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S\times A}})\Rightarrow\text{determines sampling of state-action pairs }S_{i},A_{i}\sim b_{\mu},

(28)

Stage 2:

\displaystyle\ (\Omega^{(\mathbf{N})},\Sigma^{(\mathbf{N})},\mathbb{P}^{(\mathbf{N})})\Rightarrow\text{conditioned on }(S_{i},A_{i}),\text{ determines }R_{i},S_{i}^{\prime}\sim p(\cdots|S_{i},A_{i}).

This implies that having sufficiently many observations for each
s,a
is solely associated with probability space of Stage 1. Now let us discuss how “sufficiently large”
N(s,a)
is characterized (
29
).

Temporarily assuming
N\geq 2
, we can divide the data
\mathcal{D}=\{(S_{i},A_{i},R_{i},S_{i}^{\prime})\}_{i=1}^{N}
into two halves,

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{1}=\{(S_{i},A_{i},R_{i},S_{i}^{\prime})\}_{i=1}^{\lfloor N/2\rfloor}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\mathcal{D}_{2}=\{(S_{i},A_{i},R_{i},S_{i}^{\prime})\}_{i=\lfloor N/2\rfloor+1}^{N}.

Note that we denoted observations
(S_{i},A_{i},R_{i},S_{i}^{\prime})
in capital letters, so as to indicate that they are random objects. Based on this, we define the following notations based on (
9
),

\displaystyle\mathbf{p}=\big(b_{\mu}(s,a)\big)_{s,a\in\mathcal{S\times A}}\in[0,1]^{\mathcal{S\times A}}\ \

\displaystyle\&\ \ \hat{\mathbf{p}}=\big(\hat{b}_{\mu}(s,a)\big)_{s,a\in\mathcal{S\times A}}\in[0,1]^{\mathcal{S\times A}},

and it is straightforward to see
\hat{\mathbf{p}}=\lfloor N/2\rfloor/N\cdot\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{(1)}+(N-\lfloor N/2\rfloor)/N\cdot\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{(2)}
,
where each term in the RHS is sample mean based on
\mathcal{D}_{1}
and
\mathcal{D}_{2}
, i.e.
\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{(1)}=\frac{1}{N/2}\sum_{i=1}^{N/2}\mathbf{y}_{i}^{(1)}
and
\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{(2)}=\frac{1}{N-\lfloor N/2\rfloor}\sum_{i=1}^{N-\lfloor N/2\rfloor}\mathbf{y}_{i}^{(2)}

with
\mathbf{y}_{i}^{(j)}\in\{0,1\}^{\mathcal{S\times A}}
being indicators having 1 only at the state-action pair that
(S_{i},A_{i})
correspond to in
\mathcal{D}_{j}
(
j=1,2
). Within Stage 1 probability space (
28
), we define the following subset with given
\epsilon\in(0,1]
,

\displaystyle\Omega_{\mathcal{S\times A}}^{(\epsilon)}:=\bigg\{\omega\in\Omega_{\mathcal{S\times A}}\ \bigg|\ \|\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{(1)}-\mathbf{p}\|<\frac{1}{2}p_{{\rm min}}\cdot\epsilon\quad\mbox{and}\quad\|\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{(2)}-\mathbf{p}\|<\frac{1}{2}p_{{\rm min}}\cdot\epsilon\bigg\},

(29)

under which we can verify that following holds (proofs in
C.1.3
),

Fact 1:

\displaystyle\hat{b}_{\mu}(s,a)=\frac{N(s,a)}{N}\in\bigg[\frac{1}{2}b_{\mu}(s,a),\frac{3}{2}b_{\mu}(s,a)\bigg]\text{ for }\forall s,a\in\mathcal{S\times A},

(30)

Fact 2:

\displaystyle N(s,a)\geq 2\text{ for }\forall s,a\in\mathcal{S\times A},

Fact 3:

\displaystyle\|\hat{\mathbf{p}}-\mathbf{p}\|<\frac{1}{2}p_{{\rm min}}\cdot\epsilon.

Throughout the following subsections
A.7.3
and
A.7.4
where we shall bound
\Gamma_{N}
and
\Delta_{N}
, we will resort to conditional probability measure
\mathbb{P}^{(\mathbf{N})}(\cdots):=\mathbb{P}(\cdots|\mathbf{N})
along with its corresponding sub-Gaussian norm
\|\cdot\|_{\psi_{2}(\mathbf{N})}
. In other words, we will consider
N(s,a)
to be fixed (non-random), assuming that Facts (
30
) are satisfied, and later calculate its unconditional probability with
\mathbb{P}
in
A.7.5
by Inequality (
40
).

A.7.3
Bounding Bellman Discrepancy

Once more, we would like to emphasize that
N(s,a)
are fixed, and Facts (
30
) hold. The probability space we are dealing with in this subsection is Stage 2 probability space (
28
).

Let us define the following stochastic process that can be used in bounding Bellman discrepancy
\Gamma_{N}
:

\displaystyle X_{\theta}:=\bar{\mathcal{E}}\big(\hat{\mathcal{T}}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{\theta},\mathcal{T}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{\theta}\big)\quad\mbox{and}\quad X_{\theta}(s,a):=\mathcal{E}\bigg\{\hat{\mathcal{T}}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{\theta}(s,a),\mathcal{T}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{\theta}(s,a)\bigg\},

\displaystyle\therefore\ \Gamma_{N}=\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}X_{\theta}\leq\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\bigg|X_{\theta}-X_{\theta_{0}}\bigg|+X_{\theta_{0}},

(31)

where
\theta_{0}\in\Theta
is a fixed value that will be chosen at the later in the proof.

First, let us handle the supremum term
\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}|X_{\theta}-X_{\theta_{0}}|
of Decomposition (
31
) with Dudley’s integral inequality (Theorem 8.1.6 of
Vershynin 2018
). Due to
X_{\theta}=\sum_{s,a}b_{\mu}(s,a)\cdot X_{\theta}(s,a)
,
we first need to bound the term
\|X_{\theta_{1}}(s,a)-X_{\theta_{2}}(s,a)\|_{\psi_{2}(\mathbf{N})}
. Towards that end, we can simplify it as follows,

\displaystyle X_{\theta}(s,a)=\frac{2}{N(s,a)}\sum_{i=1}^{N(s,a)}W_{i}^{\theta}-\frac{1}{N(s,a)^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{N(s,a)}\sum_{j=1}^{N(s,a)}W_{ij}^{\theta},

where
W_{i}^{\theta}
and
W_{ij}^{\theta}
are the random variables that have following realizations,

\displaystyle w_{i}^{\theta}:=\mathbb{E}\|R_{\alpha}+\gamma Z_{\alpha}(S_{\alpha}^{\prime},A_{\alpha}^{\prime};\theta)-r_{i}-\gamma Z_{\beta}(s_{i}^{\prime},A_{i}^{\prime};\theta)\|-\mathbb{E}\|R_{\alpha}+\gamma Z_{\alpha}(S_{\alpha}^{\prime},A_{\alpha}^{\prime};\theta)-R_{\beta}-\gamma Z_{\beta}(S_{\beta}^{\prime},A_{\beta}^{\prime};\theta)\|,

\displaystyle w_{ij}^{\theta}:=\mathbb{E}\|r_{i}+\gamma Z_{\alpha}(s_{i}^{\prime},A_{i}^{\prime};\theta)-r_{j}-\gamma Z_{\beta}(s_{j}^{\prime},A_{j}^{\prime};\theta)\|-\mathbb{E}\|R_{\alpha}+\gamma Z_{\alpha}(S_{\alpha}^{\prime},A_{\alpha}^{\prime};\theta)-R_{\beta}-\gamma Z_{\beta}(S_{\beta}^{\prime},A_{\beta}^{\prime};\theta)\|.

where
Z(s,a;\theta)\sim\Upsilon_{\theta}(s,a)
,
(R,S^{\prime})\sim p(\cdots|s,a)
,
(\hat{R},\hat{S}^{\prime})\sim\hat{p}(\cdots|s,a)
,
A^{\prime}\sim\pi(\cdot|S^{\prime})
,
\hat{A}^{\prime}\sim\pi(\cdot|\hat{S}^{\prime})
, and having different subscripts (
\alpha
or
\beta
) means they are independent, although they may follow the same distribution(s). Since we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}(W_{i}^{\theta})=0,\ \ \mathbb{E}(W_{ij}^{\theta})=0\ \text{if }i\neq j,\ \ \mathbb{E}(W_{ii}^{\theta})\neq 0,

we should further obtain

\displaystyle\|X_{\theta_{1}}

\displaystyle(s,a)-X_{\theta_{2}}(s,a)\|_{\psi_{2}(\mathbf{N})}\leq\frac{N(s,a)-1}{N(s,a)}\cdot\bigg\|\frac{1}{N(s,a)\cdot(N(s,a)-1)}\sum_{i\neq j}^{N(s,a)}(W_{ij}^{\theta_{1}}-W_{ij}^{\theta_{2}})\bigg\|_{\psi_{2}(\mathbf{N})}

(32)

\displaystyle\qquad+\bigg\|\frac{2}{N(s,a)}\sum_{i=1}^{N(s,a)}(W_{i}^{\theta_{1}}-W_{i}^{\theta_{2}})\bigg\|_{\psi_{2}(\mathbf{N})}+\bigg\|\frac{1}{N(s,a)^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{N(s,a)}(W_{ii}^{\theta_{1}}-W_{ii}^{\theta_{2}})\bigg\|_{\psi_{2}(\mathbf{N})},

and we will bound each term one by one. Before we begin with the first term, we would like to introduce a useful trick that will be used repetitively throughout the proof. First, it is easy to see
|W_{ij}^{\theta_{1}}-W_{ij}^{\theta_{2}}|\leq 4\gamma\cdot\mathbb{W}_{1,\infty}(\theta_{1},\theta_{2})
by Appendix
A.6
.
Defining another random variable that satisfies following based on Remark
A.1
,

\displaystyle\tilde{W}_{ij}^{\theta}:=\frac{1}{2}\cdot(W_{ij}^{\theta}+W_{ji}^{\theta})\ \ \text{where}\ \ 1\leq i<j\leq N(s,a),\quad\therefore\ \|\tilde{W}_{12}^{\theta_{1}}-\tilde{W}_{12}^{\theta_{2}}\|_{\psi_{2}(\mathbf{N})}\leq\frac{4\gamma}{\sqrt{\log 2}}\cdot\mathbb{W}_{1,\infty}(\theta_{1},\theta_{2}).

Note that we can rewrite
\sum_{i\neq j}^{N(s,a)}(W_{ij}^{\theta_{1}}-W_{ij}^{\theta_{2}})=2\cdot\sum_{i<j}^{N(s,a)}(\tilde{W}_{ij}^{\theta_{1}}-\tilde{W}_{ij}^{\theta_{2}})
, and the terms that are being added are not independent. Towards that end, we divide our cases into two, when
N(s,a)\geq 2
is an even number or an odd number. When
N(s,a)
is even, we can directly use Lemma S4 of
Wang
et al. 2022
to group
\big\{(i,j):1\leq i<j\leq N(s,a)\big\}
into
(N(s,a)-1)
groups
G_{k}\ (1\leq k\leq N(s,a)-1)
, each of which contains
|G_{k}|=N(s,a)/2
pairs of
(i,j)
, with no pair overlapping in any component. Then we have the following by Remark
A.1
,

\displaystyle\bigg\|\frac{1}{N(s,a)\cdot(N(s,a)-1)}\sum_{i\neq j}^{N(s,a)}(W_{ij}^{\theta_{1}}-W_{ij}^{\theta_{2}})\bigg\|_{\psi_{2}(\mathbf{N})}\leq\bigg\|\frac{1}{N(s,a)-1}\sum_{k=1}^{N(s,a)-1}\frac{1}{N(s,a)/2}\sum_{(i,j)\in G_{k}}(\tilde{W}_{ij}^{\theta_{1}}-\tilde{W}_{ij}^{\theta_{2}})\bigg\|_{\psi_{2}(\mathbf{N})}

\displaystyle\leq\bigg\|\frac{1}{N(s,a)/2}\sum_{(i,j)\in G_{1}}(\tilde{W}_{ij}^{\theta_{1}}-\tilde{W}_{ij}^{\theta_{2}})\bigg\|_{\psi_{2}(\mathbf{N})}\leq\frac{C_{1}}{\sqrt{N(s,a)}}\cdot\|\tilde{W}_{12}^{\theta_{1}}-\tilde{W}_{12}^{\theta_{2}}\|_{\psi_{2}(\mathbf{N})}.

(33)

Now let us assume that
N(s,a)\geq 2
is an odd number, which automatically gives us
N(s,a)\geq 3
. Then the term can be bounded as

\displaystyle\leq\frac{N(s,a)-2}{N(s,a)}\cdot\bigg\|\frac{2}{(N(s,a)-1)\cdot(N(s,a)-2)}\sum_{i<j}^{N(s,a)-1}(\tilde{W}_{ij}^{\theta_{1}}-\tilde{W}_{ij}^{\theta_{2}})\bigg\|_{\psi_{2}(\mathbf{N})}+\frac{2}{N(s,a)}\cdot\|\tilde{W}_{12}^{\theta_{1}}-\tilde{W}_{12}^{\theta_{2}}\|_{\psi_{2}(\mathbf{N})}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{2}}{\sqrt{N(s,a)}}\cdot\|\tilde{W}_{12}^{\theta_{1}}-\tilde{W}_{12}^{\theta_{2}}\|_{\psi_{2}(\mathbf{N})}\ \ \ \text{by (\ref{Wtilde_psi2_odd}) since }N(s,a)-1\text{ is an even number.}

where we used
N(s,a)-1\geq\frac{N(s,a)}{2}
for
N(s,a)\geq 3
in the last line. Then, applying the same logic to the second and third terms of Inequality (
32
), we can apply the same trick to obtain the following based on Fact (
30
),

\displaystyle\|X_{\theta_{1}}

\displaystyle-X_{\theta_{2}}\|_{\psi_{2}(\mathbf{N})}\leq\frac{C_{9}\gamma}{\sqrt{N}}\cdot\sum_{s,a}\sqrt{b_{\mu}(s,a)}\cdot\mathbb{W}_{1,\infty}(\theta_{1},\theta_{2}).

Without loss of generality, we can assume that separability holds. In addition,
\mathbb{W}_{1,\infty}
is proved to be a metric by
Bellemare
et al. 2017
in their Lemma 2. Therefore, by Assumption
3.2
, we can apply Dudley’s Integral Inequality (Theorem 8.1.6 of
Vershynin 2018
) to obtain the following for
\forall u>0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{(\mathbf{N})}\bigg[\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\bigg|X_{\theta}-X_{\theta_{0}}\bigg|\leq\frac{C_{10}\gamma}{\sqrt{N}}\cdot\sum_{s,a}\sqrt{b_{\mu}(s,a)}\cdot\bigg\{\int_{0}^{\infty}\sqrt{\log\mathcal{N}(\Theta,\mathbb{W}_{1,\infty},t)}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle+u\cdot{\rm diam}(\Theta;\mathbb{W}_{1,\infty})\bigg\}\bigg]\geq 1-2\exp(-u^{2}).

(34)

The next part is bounding the term
X_{\theta_{0}}
of Decomposition (
31
). We first fix a state-action pair
s,a\in\mathcal{S\times A}
, we can obtain the following decomposition,

\displaystyle X_{\theta_{0}}(s,a)

\displaystyle\leq 2\cdot\bigg|\frac{1}{N(s,a)}\sum_{i=1}^{N(s,a)}W_{i}^{\theta_{0}}\bigg|+\frac{N(s,a)-1}{N(s,a)}\cdot\bigg|\frac{1}{N(s,a)\cdot(N(s,a)-1)}\sum_{i\neq j}^{N(s,a)}W_{ij}^{\theta_{0}}\bigg|+\frac{1}{N(s,a)^{2}}\cdot\bigg|\sum_{i=1}^{N(s,a)}W_{ii}^{\theta_{0}}\bigg|.

We use the following

\displaystyle\big\|\mathbb{E}\|R(s,a)\|\big\|_{\psi_{2}}\leq\big\|\|R(s,a)\|\big\|_{\psi_{2}}\leq d\cdot\|R(s,a)\|_{\psi_{2}}\ \ \text{by Remark \ref{psi2_properties}},

(35)

and the following lemma
that is proved in
C.1.4
.

Lemma A.2
.

Given
X_{1},\cdots,X_{N}\sim iid\ (N\geq 2)
, let
X_{ij}:=h(X_{i},X_{j})
for some bivariate function
h(\cdot,\cdot)
, and assume that
\|X_{12}\|_{\psi_{2}}<\infty
holds. Then we have the following inequality for
\forall\epsilon>0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigg\{\bigg|\frac{1}{N(N-1)}\sum_{i\neq j}X_{ij}-\mathbb{E}(X_{12})\bigg|\geq\epsilon\bigg\}\leq 4N\cdot\exp\bigg\{\frac{-C\cdot N\cdot\epsilon^{2}}{\|X_{12}-\mathbb{E}(X_{12})\|_{\psi_{2}}^{2}}\bigg\}.

By applying Theorem 2.6.2 of
Vershynin 2018
and Lemma
A.2
, we obtain the following, skipping all technical details. For arbitrary
\epsilon_{1}>0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{(\mathbf{N})}\bigg\{X_{\theta_{0}}\leq 2\epsilon_{1}+\frac{1}{N}\cdot\frac{8}{p_{{\rm min}}}\cdot\bigg(\sup_{s,a}\mathbb{E}\|R(s,a)\|+\gamma\cdot\sup_{s,a}\mathbb{E}\|Z(s,a;\theta_{0})\|\bigg)\bigg\}

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{P}^{(\mathbf{N})}\bigg\{\sup_{s,a}X_{\theta_{0}}(s,a)\leq 2\epsilon_{1}+\frac{1}{N}\cdot\frac{8}{p_{{\rm min}}}\cdot\bigg(\sup_{s,a}\mathbb{E}\|R(s,a)\|+\gamma\cdot\sup_{s,a}\mathbb{E}\|Z(s,a;\theta_{0})\|\bigg)\bigg\}

\displaystyle\geq 1-(2|\mathcal{S\times A}|+6N)\cdot\exp\bigg\{\frac{-C_{20}\cdot p_{{\rm min}}\cdot N\cdot\epsilon_{1}^{2}}{\big(\sup_{s,a}\big\|\|R(s,a)\|\big\|_{\psi_{2}}+\gamma\cdot\sup_{s,a}\mathbb{E}\|Z(s,a;\theta_{0})\|\big)^{2}}\bigg\}.

(36)

Note that we obtained a bound for
\sup_{s,a}X_{\theta_{0}}(s,a)
, which is one step further than
X_{\theta_{0}}
. This shall be later used in proof of Lemma
B.1
for non-realizable scenario, which is suggested in
B.4
.

Now we can combine the bounds (
34
) and (
36
) to take up Decomposition (
31
) as following for
\forall u>0,\ \epsilon_{1}>0,\ N\geq 2
,
