\displaystyle\leq C_{1}\cdot\frac{1}{p_{{\rm min}}^{2}}\cdot m^{2}\cdot C_{\rm env}^{(m)}(\Theta)\cdot\bigg(\frac{1}{N^{1/4}}+\sqrt{\epsilon}\bigg)+\frac{C_{31}}{p_{{\rm min}}^{3/2}}\cdot m\cdot B_{\rm env}^{(m)}(\Theta)\cdot\bigg(\frac{1}{M^{1/4}}+\sqrt{\epsilon^{\prime}}\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{32}}{p_{{\rm min}}^{2}}\cdot B_{\rm env}^{(m)}(\Theta)\cdot\bigg\{m^{2}\cdot\bigg(\frac{1}{N^{1/4}}+\sqrt{\epsilon}\bigg)+m\cdot\bigg(\frac{1}{M^{1/4}}+\sqrt{\epsilon^{\prime}}\bigg)\bigg\}

with probability larger than

\displaystyle 1-\mathcal{D}(N)-C_{22}\cdot m\cdot(|\mathcal{S\times A}|+N)\cdot\exp(-C_{23}\cdot p_{{\rm min}}^{2}\cdot N\cdot\epsilon^{2}/C_{\rm den}(m))

\displaystyle\ \ \ -C_{22}\cdot(|\mathcal{S\times A}|+M)\cdot\exp(-C_{23}\cdot p_{{\rm min}}^{2}\cdot M\cdot{\epsilon^{\prime}}^{2}/B_{\rm den}(m)).

B.6
Final Convergence

We start from the following assumption, which is weaker than Assumption
4.3
that we stated for splitting-based estimator (Section
4.2
).

Assumption B.5
.

For some
q\geq 1
, the inaccuracy function
F(\cdot):\Theta\subset\mathbb{R}^{p}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
(
17
) satisfies
F(\theta)\geq F(\tilde{\theta})+c_{q}\cdot\|\theta-\tilde{\theta}\|^{q}
for some
c_{q}>0
.

B.6.1
Inaccuracy Of Parameter Estimation

Our idea is that larger
N,M,m
will lead to tighter (probabilistic) bound of
\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}|\hat{F}_{m}^{(B)}(\theta)-F(\theta)|
, which can be decomposed as follows,

\displaystyle\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\bigg|\hat{F}_{m}^{(B)}(\theta)-F(\theta)\bigg|\leq\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\bigg|\hat{F}_{m}^{(B)}(\theta)-F_{m}(\theta)\bigg|+\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\bigg|F_{m}(\theta)-F(\theta)\bigg|.

Note that the first term of RHS is the probabilistic term that we bounded in Lemma
B.3
, and the second term is a deterministic term that can be bounded based on following (proof in
C.1.9
), with
C_{\rm bias}
defined in (
76
),

\displaystyle\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\bigg|F_{m}(\theta)-F(\theta)\bigg|\leq 4\gamma^{m}\cdot C_{\rm bias}\quad

\displaystyle\text{with}\quad\mathbb{W}_{1,\infty}(\theta,\pi):=\mathbb{W}_{1,\infty}\big\{\Upsilon_{\theta},\Upsilon_{\pi}\big\}.

(67)

Now in order to relate this to the estimation inaccuracy of
\hat{\theta}_{m}^{(B)}
(
18
), we shall use the function
\psi(\cdot)
introduced by Example 1.3 suggested by
Sen 2018
,

\displaystyle\psi(\delta):=\inf_{\theta\in\Theta:\|\theta-\tilde{\theta}\|\geq\delta}F(\theta)-F(\tilde{\theta})\quad\mbox{and}\quad\psi^{-1}(y):=\inf\bigg\{\delta>0:\psi(\delta)\geq y\bigg\}.

(68)

Depending on whether
\Theta
includes any element in the outermost boundary,
\psi(\cdot)
can be defined either for
0\leq\delta<\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\|\theta-\tilde{\theta}\|
or
0\leq\delta\leq\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\|\theta-\tilde{\theta}\|
. We will extend the function in the following trivial way of extending horizontally from the rightmost point,

\displaystyle\psi(\delta):=\begin{cases}\psi(\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\|\theta-\tilde{\theta}\|)&\text{if }\psi(\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\|\theta-\tilde{\theta}\|)\text{ is defined,}\\
\sup_{0\leq\delta^{\prime}<\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\|\theta-\tilde{\theta}\|}\psi(\delta^{\prime})&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

There are several important properties of
\psi(\cdot)
that are proved in
C.1.10
based on Assumption
B.5
.

Remark B.6
.

The function
\psi
(
68
) satisfies the following properties.

1.

\psi^{-1}(\cdot)
is an increasing function such that
\psi^{-1}(y)\rightarrow 0
as
y\rightarrow 0
and
\psi^{-1}(y)=\infty
for
y>\sup_{\delta>0}\psi(\delta)
.

2.

\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0+}\psi\big\{\psi^{-1}(y)+\epsilon\big\}\geq y
holds for all
y\in[0,\psi(\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\|\theta-\tilde{\theta}\|)]
.

3.

Let
\hat{F}:\Theta\subset\mathbb{R}^{p}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be an arbitrary estimate of
F
(
17
) that have minimizer(s) within
\Theta
. For an arbitrary value
\delta>0
, if there exists a minimizer
\exists\hat{\theta}\in\arg\min_{\theta\in\Theta}\hat{F}(\theta)
such that
\|\hat{\theta}-\tilde{\theta}\|>\delta
, then
\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}|\hat{F}(\theta)-F(\theta)|\geq\frac{1}{2}\lim_{\delta^{\prime}\rightarrow\delta+}\psi(\delta^{\prime})
holds.

Based on the Remark
B.6
(3rd statement), we have the following bound for
\psi_{+}(\delta):=\lim_{\delta^{\prime}\rightarrow\delta+}\psi(\delta^{\prime})
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigg\{\exists\hat{\theta}_{m}^{(B)}\in\Theta\text{ such that }\|\hat{\theta}_{m}^{(B)}-

\displaystyle\tilde{\theta}\|\geq\delta\bigg\}\leq\mathbb{P}\bigg\{\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\bigg|\hat{F}_{m}^{(B)}(\theta)-F(\theta)\bigg|\geq\frac{1}{2}\psi_{+}(\delta)\bigg\}.

Now letting
\delta=\psi^{-1}\{2\cdot A(m,N,M,\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})+8\gamma^{m}\cdot C_{\rm bias}\}
where

\displaystyle A(m,N,M,\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime}):=\frac{C_{3}}{p_{{\rm min}}^{2}}\cdot B_{\rm env}^{(m)}(\Theta)\times\bigg\{m^{2}\cdot\bigg(\frac{1}{N^{1/4}}+\sqrt{\epsilon}\bigg)+m\cdot\bigg(\frac{1}{M^{1/4}}+\sqrt{\epsilon^{\prime}}\bigg)\bigg\},

(69)

we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigg\{\exists\hat{\theta}_{m}^{(B)}\in\Theta\text{ such that }\|\hat{\theta}_{m}^{(B)}-\tilde{\theta}\|\geq\psi^{-1}\bigg(2\cdot A(m,N,M,\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})+8\gamma^{m}\cdot C_{\rm bias}\bigg)\bigg\}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\bigg\{\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\bigg|\hat{F}_{m}^{(B)}(\theta)-F(\theta)\bigg|\geq A(m,N,M,\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})+4\gamma^{m}\cdot C_{\rm bias}\bigg\}\quad\text{by Remark \ref{psifunction_properties} (2nd Point)}

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{D}(N)+C_{1}\cdot m\cdot(|\mathcal{S\times A}|+N)\cdot\exp(-C_{2}\cdot p_{{\rm min}}^{2}\cdot N\cdot\epsilon^{2}/C_{\rm den}(m))

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+C_{1}\cdot(|\mathcal{S\times A}|+M)\cdot\exp(-C_{2}\cdot p_{{\rm min}}^{2}\cdot M\cdot{\epsilon^{\prime}}^{2}/B_{\rm den}(m))

(70)

Now let us simplify the bound.
Let
\delta_{1},\delta_{2}\in(0,1)
be arbitrary. Now let us simplify the result (
70
) by letting

\displaystyle\epsilon=\sqrt{\frac{C_{\rm den}(m)}{C_{2}\cdot p_{{\rm min}}^{2}}}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{1}{N}\cdot\log\bigg(\frac{C_{1}\cdot m\cdot(|\mathcal{S\times A}|+N)}{\delta_{1}}\bigg)},

\displaystyle\epsilon^{\prime}=\sqrt{\frac{B_{\rm den}(m)}{C_{2}\cdot p_{{\rm min}}^{2}}}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{1}{M}\cdot\log\bigg(\frac{C_{1}\cdot(|\mathcal{S\times A}|+M)}{\delta_{2}}\bigg)}.

(71)

Recall that we have to ensure
\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime}\in(0,1]
. Furthermore, since
A(m,N,M,\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})
in (
69
) contains
(N^{1/4}+\sqrt{\epsilon})
and
(M^{1/4}+\sqrt{\epsilon^{\prime}})
. Since
\epsilon
and
\epsilon^{\prime}
(
71
) decays in a slower rate than
N^{1/4}
and
M^{1/4}
, we can ignore them when the sample size is sufficiently large. To ensure these, we will assume
N\geq N_{m}(\delta_{1}),\ M\geq M_{m}(\delta_{2})
where
N_{m}(\delta_{1})
and
M_{m}(\delta_{2})
are the smallest integers such that
N\geq N_{m}(\delta_{1}),\ M\geq M_{m}(\delta_{2})
implies the following, with
C_{\rm den}(m)
(
59
) and
B_{\rm den}(m)
(
66
),

\displaystyle{\frac{1}{N}}\leq{\frac{C_{\rm den}(m)}{C_{2}\cdot p_{{\rm min}}^{2}}}\cdot{\frac{1}{N}\cdot\log\bigg(\frac{C_{1}\cdot m\cdot(|\mathcal{S\times A}|+N)}{\delta_{1}}\bigg)}\leq 1,

\displaystyle{\frac{1}{M}}\leq{\frac{B_{\rm den}(m)}{C_{2}\cdot p_{{\rm min}}^{2}}}\cdot{\frac{1}{M}\cdot\log\bigg(\frac{C_{1}\cdot(|\mathcal{S\times A}|+M)}{\delta_{2}}\bigg)}\leq 1.

Then let us bound the term
A(m,N,M,\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})
of Equation (
69
) as follows, using the values of
\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime}
specified in Equation (
71
) and assuming (
B.6.1
). Skipping the calculation details, we can derive

\displaystyle A(m,N,M,\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})\leq A_{1}(m,N,M,\delta_{1},\delta_{2})

\displaystyle:=C_{\rm model}\cdot\bigg[m^{2}\cdot\bigg\{\frac{1}{N}\cdot\log\bigg(\frac{C_{1}\cdot m\cdot(|\mathcal{S\times A}|+N)}{\delta_{1}}\bigg)\bigg\}^{\frac{1}{4}}+m\cdot\bigg\{\frac{1}{M}\cdot\log\bigg(\frac{C_{1}\cdot(|\mathcal{S\times A}|+M)}{\delta_{2}}\bigg)\bigg\}^{\frac{1}{4}}\bigg]

(72)

where
C_{{\rm model}}
is defined in (
76
).
Now we can rewrite Bound (
70
) as follows by Remark
B.6
(1st Statement),

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigg\{\|\hat{\theta}_{m}^{(B)}-\tilde{\theta}\|\leq\psi^{-1}\bigg(2\cdot A_{1}(m,N,M,\delta_{1},\delta_{2})+8\gamma^{m}\cdot C_{\rm bias}\bigg)\bigg\}\geq 1-\mathcal{D}(N)-\delta_{1}-\delta_{2}.

Next, we can analyze the convergence rate of our estimated distribution towards the best approximation
\Upsilon_{\tilde{\theta}}
in Energy Distance, based on the same idea that we employed in (
53
) and Assumption
4.1
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}\bigg\{\Upsilon_{\theta_{1}}(s,a),\Upsilon_{\theta_{2}}(s,a)\bigg\}

\displaystyle\leq 2\cdot\mathbb{W}_{1,\infty}(\theta_{1},\theta_{1})+2\cdot\mathbb{W}_{1,\infty}(\theta_{1},\theta_{2})\leq 2L\cdot\|\theta_{1}-\theta_{2}\|,

(73)

which leads to
\bar{\mathcal{E}}(\Upsilon_{\hat{\theta}_{m}^{(B)}},\Upsilon_{\tilde{\theta}})\leq 2L\cdot\|\hat{\theta}_{m}^{(B)}-\tilde{\theta}\|
.

B.6.2
Finite Sample Error Bound

Under Assumptions
2.1
,
3.2
,
4.1
, for any step level
m\in\mathbb{N}
and arbitrary
\delta_{1},\delta_{2}\in(0,1)
, given that
N\geq\max\{N_{m}(\delta_{1}),2\},\ M\geq\max\{M_{m}(\delta_{2}),2\}
defined in (
B.6.1
), we have the following bound with probability larger than
1-\mathcal{D}(N)-\delta_{1}-\delta_{2}
, all values of
\hat{\theta}_{m}^{(B)}\in\arg\min_{\theta\in\Theta}\hat{F}_{m}^{(B)}(\theta)
satisfy

\displaystyle\|\hat{\theta}_{m}^{(B)}-\tilde{\theta}\|\leq\psi^{-1}\bigg(2C_{{\rm model}}\cdot\bigg[m^{2}\cdot\bigg\{\frac{1}{N}\cdot\log\bigg(\frac{C_{1}\cdot m\cdot(|\mathcal{S\times A}|+N)}{\delta_{1}}\bigg)\bigg\}^{\frac{1}{4}}

(74)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+m\cdot\bigg\{\frac{1}{M}\cdot\log\bigg(\frac{C_{1}\cdot(|\mathcal{S\times A}|+M)}{\delta_{2}}\bigg)\bigg\}^{\frac{1}{4}}\bigg]+4\gamma^{m}\cdot C_{{\rm bias}}\bigg),

along with

\displaystyle\bar{\mathcal{E}}\big(\Upsilon_{\hat{\theta}_{m}^{(B)}},\Upsilon_{\tilde{\theta}}\big)\leq 2L\cdot\|\hat{\theta}_{m}^{(B)}-\tilde{\theta}\|,

(75)

where
\mathcal{D}(N)\rightarrow 0
as
N\rightarrow\infty
(
76
), and
\psi^{-1}
(
68
) is an increasing function that ensures
\psi^{-1}(y)\rightarrow 0
as
y\rightarrow 0
, as stated in Remark
B.6
(1st statement).

Here is the recap of definitions of the terms that we used, along with
V(s,a):=\exp(\|R(s,a)\|^{2}/t_{0}(s,a)^{2})
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(N):=|\mathcal{S\times A}|\cdot\frac{\sqrt{2}}{\sqrt{p_{{\rm min}}}}\cdot\inf_{r>2}\bigg\{N^{\frac{1}{r}-\frac{1}{2}}+\sup_{s,a}\mathbb{E}\big\{V(s,a)\cdot\mathbf{1}\big(V(s,a)>N^{1/r}\big)\big\}\bigg\}\rightarrow 0\text{ as }N\rightarrow\infty

\displaystyle C_{{\rm bias}}:=\mathbb{W}_{1,\infty}(\tilde{\theta},\pi)+L\cdot{\rm diam}(\Theta;\|\cdot\|),

\displaystyle C_{{\rm model}}:=\frac{C_{4}}{p_{{\rm min}}^{5/2}}\bigg\{L^{2}p{\rm diam}(\Theta;\|\cdot\|)^{2}+\bigg(\frac{1}{1-\gamma}\bigg)^{2}\max\big\{d\cdot\sup_{s,a}\|R(s,a)\|_{\psi_{2}},t_{0}^{*}\big\}^{2}+\big(\sup_{s,a}\mathbb{E}\|Z(s,a;\theta_{0})\|\big)^{2}+1\bigg\}^{\frac{3}{4}},

\displaystyle t_{0}^{*}:=\sup_{s,a}t_{0}(s,a)\ \ \text{where }t_{0}(s,a)>0\ \text{are values such that }\ \mathbb{E}\bigg\{\exp\bigg(\frac{\|R(s,a)\|^{2}}{t_{0}(s,a)^{2}}\bigg)\bigg\}=1.

(76)

Just for a brief note, minimizer(s) of the estimated objective function
\hat{\theta}_{m}^{(B)}\in\arg\min_{\theta\in\Theta}\hat{F}_{m}^{(B)}(\theta)
always exists due to continuity of
\hat{F}_{m}^{(B)}
(proof in
C.1.11
) and closedness of
\Theta
mentioned in Assumption
4.1
, which also implies
{\rm diam}(\Theta;\|\cdot\|)<\infty
.

B.6.3
Asymptotic Setting

Based on the finite-sample error bound provided in
B.6.2
, we will now assume
N,M,m
are large enough to satisfy the assumptions
N\geq\max\{N_{m}(\delta_{1}),2\},\ M\geq\max\{M_{m}(\delta_{2}),2\}
. We should also assume the following holds as
N,M,m\rightarrow\infty
(terms defined in (
72
) and (
67
)),

\displaystyle 2\cdot A_{1}(m,N,M,\delta_{1},\delta_{2})+8\gamma^{m}\cdot C_{\rm bias}\rightarrow 0

(77)

where the LHS is exactly the term inside
\psi^{-1}(\cdot)
in (
74
). This condition is necessary to ensure that the RHS of Bound (
74
) to have a finite value by Remark
B.6
(1st statement). It should be verified that these conditions hold in the asymptotic sense, which we will discuss in the following section
B.6.4
where we choose the actual growing speed of
M
and
m
with respect to
N
.

By Assumption
B.5
, we could derive the following within the proof of Remark
B.6
in
C.1.10
(1st statement),

\displaystyle\psi^{-1}(y)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{{c_{q}}^{1/q}}\cdot y^{1/q}\ \ \ \text{for }\forall y\in[0,\sup_{\delta>0}\psi(\delta))\quad\text{by \eqref{psiinverse_upperbound_technique}}.

Based on this, by letting
\delta_{1}=\delta_{2}=\delta/2
in (
74
), we have following with probability larger than
1-\mathcal{D}(N)-\delta
,

\displaystyle\|\hat{\theta}_{m}^{(B)}-\tilde{\theta}\|\lesssim\bigg[\underbrace{m^{2}\cdot\bigg\{\frac{1}{N}\cdot\log\bigg(\frac{2mN}{\delta}\bigg)\bigg\}^{\frac{1}{4}}}_{\mathrm{data}}+\underbrace{m\cdot\bigg\{\frac{1}{M}\log\bigg(\frac{2M}{\delta}\bigg)\bigg\}^{\frac{1}{4}}}_{\mathrm{bootstrap}}+\underbrace{\gamma^{m}}_{\mathrm{bias}}\bigg]^{\frac{1}{q}},

(78)

where
\lesssim
means bounded by the given bound (RHS) multiplied by a positive number that does not depend on
N,M,m
.
Each of the three terms (
78
) corresponds to the inaccuracy associated with the observed data
\{(s_{i},a_{i},r_{i},s_{i}^{\prime})\}_{i=1}^{N}
, the resampled trajectories (
51
), and the extent of non-realizability. Each can be reduced by increasing
N
,
M
, and
m
, however larger
m
makes the first two terms more challenging for to shrink, so it resembles bias-variance trade-off.

Accordingly to (
78
), the conditions of
N,M,m
(
77
) can be rewritten as following,

\displaystyle N\geq\max\{N_{m}(\delta/2),2\},\quad M\geq\max\{M_{m}(\delta/2),2\},

(79)

based on Definitions (
B.6.1
), along with the following based on Definition (
72
),

\displaystyle 2C_{{\rm model}}\cdot\bigg[m^{2}\cdot\bigg\{\frac{1}{N}\cdot\log\bigg(\frac{2C_{1}\cdot m\cdot(|\mathcal{S\times A}|+N)}{\delta}\bigg)\bigg\}^{\frac{1}{4}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad+m\cdot\bigg\{\frac{1}{M}\cdot\log\bigg(\frac{2C_{1}\cdot(|\mathcal{S\times A}|+M)}{\delta}\bigg)\bigg\}^{\frac{1}{4}}\bigg]+8\gamma^{m}\cdot C_{{\rm bias}}\rightarrow 0.

(80)

B.6.4
Optimal Convergence Rate

With general
q\geq 1
(instead of
q=2
), we can increase
M
and
m
with the sample size
N
to obtain the following convergence rate towards the best-approximation represented by
\tilde{\theta}
. The following theorem suggests the optimal way of choosing
M
and
m
so that it converges fastest as
N
grows.

Theorem B.7
.

Under Assumptions
2.1
,
3.2
,
4.1
,
B.5
, letting
M=\lfloor C_{1}\cdot N\rfloor
and
m=\lfloor\frac{1}{4}\log_{(1/\gamma)}(C_{2}N/\log N)\rfloor
for any positive
constants
C_{1},C_{2}>0
, we have the following convergence rate of the upper bound

\displaystyle\bar{\mathcal{E}}\big(\Upsilon_{\hat{\theta}_{m}^{(B)}},\Upsilon_{\tilde{\theta}}\big)\leq\tilde{O}_{p}\bigg[\frac{1}{N^{1/(4q)}}\cdot\bigg\{\log_{\frac{1}{\gamma}}\bigg(\frac{N}{\log N}\bigg)\bigg\}^{2/q}\bigg],

where
\tilde{O}_{p}
indicates the rate of convergence up to logarithmic order. Letting
q=2
gives us the convergence rate of
\tilde{O}_{p}(1/N^{1/8})
.

Now we will assume an asymptotic case where the sample size grows to infinity
N\rightarrow\infty
, and
M,m
grow accordingly with a chosen rate. Towards that end, we have to achieve two goals. First, we have to ensure that (
79
) and (
80
) are satisfied as
N\rightarrow\infty
. Second, we have to make (
78
) shrink in the fastest possible rate. As long as
M
(which we can let to be arbitrarily large) grows in the same rate with (or faster than)
N
,

\displaystyle M=\lfloor C_{5}\cdot N\rfloor\quad\text{for arbitrary}\quad C_{5}>0,

(81)

the second term of (
78
) becomes ignorable in the asymptotic sense.

As noted below (
78
), increasing
m
has trade-off effect, so we shall derive an appropriate speed of
m
, and then verify that it can satisfy (
79
) with large enough
N
. Using (
81
), we can take up (
78
) as follows for sufficiently large
N
,

\displaystyle\|\hat{\theta}_{m}^{(B)}-\tilde{\theta}\|\lesssim\bigg[m^{2}\cdot\bigg\{\frac{1}{N}\cdot\log\bigg(\frac{mN}{\delta}\bigg)\bigg\}^{\frac{1}{4}}+\gamma^{m}\bigg]^{\frac{1}{q}}.

Now we choose the optimal level of
m
that makes the two terms converge in the same rate,

\displaystyle\gamma^{m}\approx m^{2}\cdot\frac{1}{N^{1/4}}\cdot\big(\log(mN)\big)^{1/4}.

However, this relationship is very intricate, so we could not calculate
m
that makes both sides perfectly match. So we alternatively solved an easier equation that gives us the following relationship,

\displaystyle\gamma^{m}\approx C_{6}\cdot\bigg(\frac{\log N}{N}\bigg)^{1/4},\quad\therefore\ m\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{let}}\bigg\lfloor\frac{1}{4}\cdot\log_{\frac{1}{\gamma}}\bigg(\frac{C_{7}\cdot N}{\log N}\bigg)\bigg\rfloor.

(82)

Note that the values of
C_{6},C_{7}>0
can be arbitrary, as long as
C_{7}=C_{6}^{-4}
holds. Skipping the calculation details, it can be ascertained that the orders (
81
) and (
82
) ensures (
79
) and (
80
) to hold as
N\rightarrow\infty
. This can be verified based on the fact that
\sup_{m\in\mathbb{N}}C_{\rm den}(m)<\infty
and
\sup_{m\in\mathbb{N}}B_{\rm den}(m)<\infty
, which are defined in (
59
) and (
66
).

Furthermore, it allows us to achieve

\displaystyle\bigg\{\frac{m^{8}}{N}\cdot\log\bigg(

\displaystyle\frac{mN}{\delta}\bigg)\bigg\}^{\frac{1}{4}}\lesssim\frac{1}{N^{\frac{1}{4}}}\cdot\bigg\{\log_{\frac{1}{\gamma}}\bigg(\frac{C_{8}N}{\log N}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{4}}\bigg\}^{2}\cdot\bigg(\log\bigg[N\cdot\bigg\{\log_{\frac{1}{\gamma}}\bigg(\frac{C_{8}N}{\log N}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{4}}\bigg\}\bigg/\delta\bigg]\bigg)^{\frac{1}{4}},

which eventually leads to following when combined with (
75
),

\displaystyle\bar{\mathcal{E}}\big(\Upsilon_{\hat{\theta}_{m}^{(B)}},\Upsilon_{\tilde{\theta}}\big)\leq\tilde{O}_{p}\bigg[\frac{1}{N^{1/(4q)}}\cdot\bigg\{\log_{\frac{1}{\gamma}}\bigg(\frac{N}{\log N}\bigg)\bigg\}^{2/q}\cdot\bigg[\log\bigg\{N\cdot\log_{\frac{1}{\gamma}}\bigg(\frac{N}{\log N}\bigg)\bigg\}\bigg]^{1/(4q)}\bigg],

which gives us desired result of Theorem
B.7
.

B.7
Splitting-based Estimator

Then a natural question arises for the bootstrap-based estimator. “In case of using
m=1
and assuming realizability, does Theorem
B.7
degenerate to the convergence rate
\tilde{O}_{p}(\sqrt{\log N/N})
shown in Theorem
3.3
?” Superficially, it seems not. If we put
q=2
(which is generally assumed in theoretical studies), we obtain a much slower rate of convergence
\tilde{O}_{p}(N^{1/8}\cdot\log(N/\log N))
. However, this is mainly since we resorted to a different proof structure that can be applied when samples are re-used (bootstrap).

Assume that we split the
|\mathcal{D}|=N
observations into
m
equally-sliced subdatasets, say
|\mathcal{D}_{1}|=\cdots=|\mathcal{D}_{m}|=N/m
(assuming
N/m
to be an integer for convenience). Instead of bootstrapping, we will use each
\mathcal{D}_{j}
(
j=1,\cdots,m
) separately to estimate Bellman operator at the
j
-th step, denoting them as
\hat{\mathcal{T}}_{j}^{\pi}
. Of course the population Bellman operator is the same for every step
j=1,\cdots,m
, however their estimate will be different in our case. With any step level
m
, the population objective function
F_{m}(\cdot)
stays the same as (
17
). On the contrary, the estimated objective function should be modified into

\displaystyle\hat{F}_{m}(\theta):=\hat{\bar{\mathcal{E}}}(\Upsilon_{\theta},\hat{\mathcal{T}}_{1:m}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{\theta})\quad\text{where }\quad\hat{\mathcal{T}}_{1:m}^{\pi}=\hat{\mathcal{T}}_{1}^{\pi}\cdots\hat{\mathcal{T}}_{m}^{\pi},

(83)

where each
\hat{\mathcal{T}}_{j}^{\pi}:\mathcal{P}^{\mathcal{S\times A}}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}^{\mathcal{S\times A}}
is based on
\hat{p}_{j}
estimated from
|\mathcal{D}_{j}|
(
j=1,\cdots,m
).

B.7.1
Convergence With An Arbitrary Step Level

We will first fix our step level and then analyze the convergence rate of our estimator. In addition, we have to assume strong convexity of our population objective function
F_{m}(\cdot)
.

Assumption B.8
.

We have

F_{m}(\theta)\geq F_{m}(\theta_{*}^{(m)})+c\cdot\|\theta-\theta_{*}^{(m)}\|^{2}
for the unique minimizer
\theta_{*}^{(m)}
.

Then, we obtain the following result for realizability and non-realizability, respectively.

Lemma B.9
.

Under Assumptions
2.1
,
3.2
,
4.1
,
B.8
, for any level
m
, the estimator (
83
) satisfies the following result regardless of realizability,

\displaystyle\bar{\mathcal{E}}(\Upsilon_{\hat{\theta}^{(m)}},\Upsilon_{\theta_{*}^{(m)}})\leq\frac{|\mathcal{S\times A}|}{\sqrt{p_{\rm min}}}\cdot L(L\sqrt{p}+\sqrt{d})\cdot O_{p}\bigg(\sqrt{\frac{m^{3}}{N}}\bigg).

We can see that the asymptotic convergence rate under realizability with single-step estimator (
m=1
) becomes
O_{p}(1/\sqrt{N})
, which can be seen as successful degeneration into Theorem
3.3
. In fact, it is slightly faster than
O_{p}(\sqrt{\log N/N})
(claimed by Theorem
3.3
) by logarithmic scale. This is not strange, since we have made additional assumptions. In addition,
\gamma
also plays a role in the realizable case via
1/(1-\gamma)
, and it is included in the
O_{p}
term.

B.7.2
Proof Of Lemma
B.9

We will resort to the modified version of peeling argument (Theorem
C.1
). Its proof follows the same structure with Theorems 5.1 and 5.2 stated by
Sen 2018
. Then the term
W_{N}(\delta)
is defined as follows,

\displaystyle W_{N}(\delta):=\sup_{\theta:\|\theta-\theta_{*}^{(m)}\|\leq\delta}\bigg|(\hat{F}_{m}(\theta)-F_{m}(\theta))-(\hat{F}_{m}(\theta_{*}^{(m)})-F_{m}(\theta_{*}^{(m)}))\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\theta:\|\theta-\theta_{*}^{(m)}\|\leq\delta}\bigg|\hat{\bar{\mathcal{E}}}(\Upsilon_{\theta},\hat{\mathcal{T}}_{1:m}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{\theta})-\bar{\mathcal{E}}(\Upsilon_{\theta},(\mathcal{T}^{\pi})^{m}\Upsilon_{\theta})-\hat{\bar{\mathcal{E}}}(\Upsilon_{\theta_{*}^{(m)}},\hat{\mathcal{T}}_{1:m}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{\theta_{*}^{(m)}})+\bar{\mathcal{E}}(\Upsilon_{\theta_{*}^{(m)}},(\mathcal{T}^{\pi})^{m}\Upsilon_{\theta_{*}^{(m)}})\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\theta:\|\theta-\theta_{*}^{(m)}\|\leq\delta}\bigg|\hat{\bar{\mathcal{E}}}(\Upsilon_{\theta},\hat{\mathcal{T}}_{1:m}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{\theta})-\hat{\bar{\mathcal{E}}}(\Upsilon_{\theta_{*}^{(m)}},\hat{\mathcal{T}}_{1:m}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{\theta_{*}^{(m)}})-\bar{\mathcal{E}}(\Upsilon_{\theta},\hat{\mathcal{T}}_{1:m}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{\theta})+\bar{\mathcal{E}}(\Upsilon_{\theta_{*}^{(m)}},\hat{\mathcal{T}}_{1:m}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{\theta_{*}^{(m)}})\bigg|

(84)

\displaystyle\quad+\sup_{\theta:\|\theta-\theta_{*}^{(m)}\|\leq\delta}\bigg|\bar{\mathcal{E}}(\Upsilon_{\theta},\hat{\mathcal{T}}_{1:m}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{\theta})-\bar{\mathcal{E}}(\Upsilon_{\theta_{*}^{(m)}},\hat{\mathcal{T}}_{1:m}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{\theta_{*}^{(m)}})-\bar{\mathcal{E}}(\Upsilon_{\theta},(\mathcal{T}^{\pi})^{m}\Upsilon_{\theta})+\bar{\mathcal{E}}(\Upsilon_{\theta_{*}^{(m)}},(\mathcal{T}^{\pi})^{m}\Upsilon_{\theta_{*}^{(m)}})\bigg|.

(85)

Our goal is to bound the expectation of two terms (
84
) and (
85
) as follows. But prior to that, let us split the cases into two:

1.

For
\forall j\in\{1,\cdots,m\}
,
\forall s,a\in\mathcal{S\times A}
, we have
|\frac{N_{j}(s,a)}{N/m}-b_{\mu}(s,a)|\leq\frac{1}{2}p_{\rm min}
, where
N_{j}(s,a)
is the number of observations of
s,a
within
|\mathcal{D}_{j}|
.

2.

Otherwise.

We will denote the first event as
\Omega_{0}
and its conditioned expectation as
\mathbb{E}_{0}(\cdots):=\mathbb{E}(\cdots|\Omega_{0})
. For a fixed
j\in\{1,\cdots,m\}
, letting
\hat{\mathbf{p}}^{(j)}
be defined accordingly to Section
A.7.2
based on the
j
-th subdataset
\mathcal{D}_{j}
, we have the following based on Lemma
A.3
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigg\{\Omega_{0}^{c}\bigg\}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mathbb{P}\bigg\{\|\hat{\mathbf{p}}^{(j)}-\mathbf{p}\|\geq\frac{1}{2}p_{\rm min}\bigg\}\leq m\cdot\exp\bigg(\frac{1}{4}\bigg)\cdot\exp\bigg(\frac{-\frac{N}{m}\cdot\frac{1}{4}p_{\rm min}^{2}}{32}\bigg)\leq m\cdot O\bigg(\exp\big(\frac{-N}{m}\big)\bigg).

(86)

Now let us bound the expectation of (
85
). First, let us condition upon
\Omega_{0}
.

\displaystyle\eqref{WNdelta_BD}\leq\sum_{s,a}b_{\mu}(s,a)\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}B_{j}(s,a),

where
B_{j}(s,a)
represents the following term

\displaystyle\sup_{\theta:\|\theta-\theta_{*}^{(m)}\|\leq\delta}\bigg|\mathcal{E}\bigg\{\Upsilon_{\theta}(s,a),\hat{\mathcal{T}}_{1:j}^{\pi}(\mathcal{T}^{\pi})^{m-j}\Upsilon_{\theta}(s,a)\bigg\}-\mathcal{E}\bigg\{\Upsilon_{\theta_{*}^{(m)}}(s,a),\hat{\mathcal{T}}_{1:j}^{\pi}(\mathcal{T}^{\pi})^{m-j}\Upsilon_{\theta_{*}^{(m)}}(s,a)\bigg\}

\displaystyle\ \ \ -\mathcal{E}\bigg\{\Upsilon_{\theta}(s,a),\hat{\mathcal{T}}_{1:j+1}^{\pi}(\mathcal{T}^{\pi})^{m-j-1}\Upsilon_{\theta}(s,a)\bigg\}+\mathcal{E}\bigg\{\Upsilon_{\theta_{*}^{(m)}}(s,a),\hat{\mathcal{T}}_{1:j+1}^{\pi}(\mathcal{T}^{\pi})^{m-j-1}\Upsilon_{\theta_{*}^{(m)}}(s,a)\bigg\}\bigg|.

Here, for
j=0
, we observe
\hat{\mathcal{T}}_{1:0}
, and this can be understood as the identity operator. One important fact is that
\mathcal{D}_{1},\cdots\mathcal{D}_{j-1}
, which
\hat{\mathcal{T}}_{1}^{\pi},\cdots,\hat{\mathcal{T}}_{j-1}^{\pi}
are based upon, are independent from
\mathcal{D}_{j}
. By applying the same logic that we used in
A.7.3
(i.e. employing the trick
A.6
), it comes down to bounding the following term with
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}_{0}
being the conditional expectation upon
\mathcal{D}_{1},\cdots\mathcal{D}_{j-1}
:

\displaystyle\sup_{\theta:\|\theta-\theta_{*}^{(m)}\|\leq\delta}\bigg|\frac{1}{N_{j}(s,a)}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{j}(s,a)}f_{i}^{\theta}-\tilde{\mathbb{E}}_{0}(f_{i}^{\theta})\bigg|+\sup_{\theta:\|\theta-\theta_{*}^{(m)}\|\leq\delta}\bigg|\frac{1}{N_{j}(s,a)^{2}}\sum_{i_{1}=1}^{N_{j}(s,a)}\sum_{i_{2}=1}^{N_{j}(s,a)}f_{i_{1}i_{2}}^{\theta}-\tilde{\mathbb{E}}_{0}(f_{12}^{\theta})\bigg|.

for some
f_{i}^{\theta}
and
f_{i_{1}i_{2}}^{\theta}
such that

\displaystyle|f_{i}^{\theta_{1}}-f_{i}^{\theta_{2}}|,|f_{i_{1}i_{2}}^{\theta_{1}}-f_{i_{1}i_{2}}^{\theta_{2}}|\leq 4(1+\gamma^{m})\cdot\mathbb{W}_{1,\infty}(\theta_{1},\theta_{2})\ \text{for }i_{1}\neq i_{2},

\displaystyle\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}|f_{ii}^{\theta}-\tilde{\mathbb{E}}_{0}(f_{12}^{\theta})|=O(1).

Then, by Theorem 8.1.3 of
Vershynin 2018
, which is expectation-version of Dudley’s integral inequality, we obtain

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbb{E}}_{0}\big\{B_{j}(s,a)\big\}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{1}}{\sqrt{\min_{s,a}N_{j}(s,a)}}\cdot\int_{0}^{\infty}\sqrt{\log\mathcal{N}(N_{\|\cdot\|}(\theta_{*}^{(m)},\delta),\mathbb{W}_{1,\infty},\epsilon)}\mathrm{d}\epsilon+\frac{d}{1-\gamma}\cdot\mathcal{O}\bigg(\frac{1}{\min_{s,a}N_{j}(s,a)}\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{L\sqrt{p}}{\sqrt{p_{\rm min}}}\cdot\delta\cdot O\bigg(\frac{1}{\sqrt{N/m}}\bigg)+\frac{1}{p_{\rm min}}\cdot\frac{d}{1-\gamma}\cdot O\bigg(\frac{1}{N/m}\bigg)

where the second term in the first line is due to the diagonal terms
f_{ii}-\tilde{\mathbb{E}}(f_{12}^{\theta})
, and the second line is based on Remark
A.5
(based on Assumption
4.1
). This further leads to

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{0}\big\{\eqref{WNdelta_BD}\big\}\leq\frac{|\mathcal{S\times A}|}{\sqrt{p_{\rm min}}}\cdot L\sqrt{p}\cdot\delta\cdot O\bigg(\sqrt{\frac{m^{3}}{N}}\bigg)+\frac{1}{p_{\rm min}}\cdot\frac{d}{1-\gamma}\cdot O\bigg(\frac{m^{2}}{N}\bigg).

Due to Assumption
3.2
, it can be bounded under
\Omega_{0}^{c}
, that is
\eqref{WNdelta_BD}\leq O(1)
. Then, combined with (
86
), we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big\{\eqref{WNdelta_BD}\big\}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{p_{\rm min}}}\cdot L\sqrt{p}\cdot\delta\cdot O\bigg(\sqrt{\frac{m^{3}}{N}}\bigg)+\frac{1}{p_{\rm min}}\cdot\frac{d}{1-\gamma}\cdot O\bigg(\frac{m^{2}}{N}\bigg)+O\bigg(\exp\big(\frac{-N}{m}\big)\bigg)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{p_{\rm min}}}\cdot L\sqrt{p}\cdot\delta\cdot O\bigg(\sqrt{\frac{m^{3}}{N}}\bigg)+\frac{1}{p_{\rm min}}\cdot\frac{d}{1-\gamma}\cdot O\bigg(\frac{m^{2}}{N}\bigg).

Now let us bound the expectation of (
84
).

\displaystyle\eqref{WNdelta_SD}\leq\sup_{\theta:\|\theta-\theta_{*}^{(m)}\|\leq\delta}\bigg|\sum_{s,a}(\hat{b}_{\mu}(s,a)-b_{\mu}(s,a))\cdot\mathcal{E}\bigg\{\Upsilon_{\theta}(s,a),\hat{\mathcal{T}}_{1:m}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{\theta}(s,a)\bigg\}-\mathcal{E}\bigg\{\Upsilon_{\theta_{0}}(s,a),\hat{\mathcal{T}}_{1:m}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{\theta_{0}}(s,a)\bigg\}\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{s,a}\bigg|b_{\mu}(s,a)-\hat{b}_{\mu}(s,a)\bigg|\cdot\sup_{\theta:\|\theta-\theta_{*}^{(m)}\|\leq\delta}\sup_{s,a}\bigg|\mathcal{E}\bigg\{\Upsilon_{\theta}(s,a),\hat{\mathcal{T}}_{1:m}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{\theta}(s,a)\bigg\}-\mathcal{E}\bigg\{\Upsilon_{\theta_{0}}(s,a),\hat{\mathcal{T}}_{1:m}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{\theta_{0}}(s,a)\bigg\}\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{s,a}\bigg|b_{\mu}(s,a)-\hat{b}_{\mu}(s,a)\bigg|\cdot\sup_{\theta:\|\theta-\theta_{*}^{(m)}\|\leq\delta}4\cdot(1+\gamma^{m})\cdot\mathbb{W}_{1,\infty}(\theta,\theta_{*}^{(m)})\quad\text{by technique \eqref{energy_difference_trick}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{s,a}\bigg|b_{\mu}(s,a)-\hat{b}_{\mu}(s,a)\bigg|\cdot 8L\cdot\delta.

Using the terms
\mathbf{p}
and
\hat{\mathbf{p}}
introduced in Section
A.7.2
, we can apply the technique that we used in Section
A.7.4
as follows,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg\{\sum_{s,a}\bigg|b_{\mu}(s,a)-\hat{b}_{\mu}(s,a)\bigg|\bigg\}=\mathbb{E}\big(\|\hat{\mathbf{p}}-\mathbf{p}\|_{1}\big)\leq\sqrt{|\mathcal{S\times A}|}\cdot\mathbb{E}\big(\|\hat{\mathbf{p}}-\mathbf{p}\|\big)

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{|\mathcal{S\times A}|}\cdot\mathbb{E}\big(\|\hat{\mathbf{p}}-\mathbf{p}\|^{2}\big)^{1/2}\leq\sqrt{|\mathcal{S\times A}|}\cdot\bigg\{\int_{0}^{1}\mathbb{P}\big(\|\hat{\mathbf{p}}-\mathbf{p}\|^{2}\geq t\big)\mathrm{d}t\bigg\}^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq O\bigg(\sqrt{\frac{|\mathcal{S\times A}|}{N}}\bigg).

Then, we finally have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big\{\eqref{WNdelta_SD}\big\}\leq 8L\cdot\sqrt{|\mathcal{S\times A}|}\cdot\delta\cdot O\bigg(\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\bigg).

Putting together (
84
) and (
85
), and using
p_{\rm min}\leq 1/|\mathcal{S\times A}|
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big\{W_{N}(\delta)\big\}\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{p_{\rm min}}}\cdot L\sqrt{p}\cdot\delta\cdot O\bigg(\sqrt{\frac{m^{3}}{N}}\bigg)+\frac{1}{p_{\rm min}}\cdot\frac{d}{1-\gamma}\cdot O\bigg(\frac{m^{2}}{N}\bigg).

Now we can apply the peeling argument (Theorem
C.1
). We will skip the proof for
\hat{\theta}^{(m)}\rightarrow^{P}\theta_{*}^{(m)}
, since this is analogous to the proof structure in (
74
). Since we have
\alpha=1
and
q=2
by Assumption
B.8
, we have

\displaystyle\|\hat{\theta}^{(m)}-\theta_{*}^{(m)}\|\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{p_{\rm min}}}\cdot\bigg(L\sqrt{p}+\sqrt{\frac{d}{1-\gamma}}\bigg)\cdot O_{p}\bigg(\sqrt{\frac{m^{3}}{N}}\bigg).

Applying the logic of (
73
), we obtain the desired result. The quantities
\sup_{s,a}\|R(s,a)\|_{\psi_{2}}
and
\sup_{s,a}\mathbb{E}\|Z(s,a;\theta_{*}^{(m)})\|
is hidden in the term
O_{p}(1)
of Lemma
B.9
.

B.7.3
Proof Of Theorem
4.4

Its proof is simple. By (
24
), we have
\bar{\mathcal{E}}(\Upsilon_{\hat{\theta}^{(m)}},\Upsilon_{\tilde{\theta}})\leq 2\cdot\{\bar{\mathcal{E}}(\Upsilon_{\hat{\theta}^{(m)}},\Upsilon_{\theta_{*}^{(m)}})+\bar{\mathcal{E}}(\Upsilon_{\theta_{*}^{(m)}},\Upsilon_{\tilde{\theta}})\}
. By (
73
), we have
\bar{\mathcal{E}}(\Upsilon_{\theta_{*}^{(m)}},\Upsilon_{\tilde{\theta}})\leq 2L\cdot\|\theta_{*}^{(m)}-\tilde{\theta}\|
. We can employ the same technique (
78
) to obtain

\displaystyle\|\theta_{*}^{(m)}-\tilde{\theta}\|\leq\bigg(\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\bigg|F_{m}(\theta)-F(\theta)\bigg|\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq O(\gamma^{\frac{m}{2}})\quad\text{by \eqref{Fact1to2}}.

Now letting
m=\lfloor\log_{\frac{1}{\gamma}}(C\cdot N)\rfloor
for an arbitrary
C>0
, we obtain the desired rate.

Appendix C
SUPPORTING RESULTS

C.1
Supporting Theoretical Results

C.1.1
Modified Version Of Peeling Argument

We can slightly modify the standard proof structure of peeling argument (e.g. see the proof of Theorem 5.1 of
Sen 2018
) to obtain the following.

Theorem C.1
.

(Peeling argument modified from Theorem 5.1 of
Sen 2018
) For a metric space
(\Theta,d)
, assuming that
\theta_{0}\in\Theta
is the unique minimizer of
M(\theta)
that is constructed based on a loss function
l_{\theta}(x)
, we define

\displaystyle M(\theta)=\mathbb{E}l_{\theta}(X),\quad\theta_{0}=\arg\min_{\theta\in\Theta}M(\theta),\quad M_{n}(\theta)=\frac{1}{n}l_{\theta}(X_{i}),\quad\hat{\theta}_{n}=\arg\min_{\theta\in\Theta}M(\theta_{0}),

\displaystyle\Delta_{n}(\theta)=(M_{n}(\theta)-M(\theta))-(M_{n}(\theta_{0})-M(\theta_{0})),\quad W_{n}(\delta)=\sup_{d(\theta,\theta_{0})\leq\delta}|\Delta_{n}(\theta)|.

Assume that there exists some
q\geq 1
that satisfies

\displaystyle M(\theta)\geq M(\theta_{0})+\lambda d^{q}(\theta,\theta_{0}),\quad\mathbb{E}\big\{W_{n}(\delta)\big\}\leq\frac{c_{1}\cdot\delta^{\alpha}}{\sqrt{n}}+c_{2}\cdot d_{n}\quad\text{for some }\alpha\in(0,q).

Given that
\hat{\theta}_{n}\rightarrow^{P}\theta_{0}
, we have
d(\hat{\theta}_{n},\theta_{0})=c_{1}^{\frac{1}{q-\alpha}}\cdot O_{p}\{(1/n)^{\frac{1}{2(q-\alpha)}}\}+c_{2}^{\frac{1}{q}}\cdot O_{p}(d_{n}^{\frac{1}{q}})
.

C.1.2
Proof Of Remark
A.1

Properties 1 and 2 are mentioned in Example 2.5.8 suggested by
Vershynin 2018
. Property 3 follows directly by using
{\mathbb{E}(X)^{2}}/{t^{2}}\leq\mathbb{E}({X^{2}}/{t^{2}})
.
Property 4 can be verified as follows. Let
\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},\cdots,x_{d})^{\intercal}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with unit norm
\|\mathbf{x}\|=1
be arbitrary. Denoting the canonical vectors as
\mathbf{e}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{e}_{d}
, we have the following by (
25
) and
|x_{j}|\leq 1
,

\displaystyle\big|\langle\mathbf{X},\mathbf{x}\rangle\big|

\displaystyle=\big|\langle\mathbf{X},x_{1}\mathbf{e}_{1}\rangle+\cdots+\langle\mathbf{X},x_{d}\mathbf{e}_{d}\rangle\big|\leq|x_{1}|\cdot\|\mathbf{X}\|_{\psi_{2}}+\cdots+|x_{d}|\cdot\|\mathbf{X}\|_{\psi_{2}}\leq d\|\mathbf{X}\|_{\psi_{2}}

Property 5 is verified in Exercise 2.7.10 suggested by
Vershynin 2018
. Property 6 is straightforward from Proposition 2.6.1 of
Vershynin 2018
.

C.1.3
Proof Of Facts (
30
)

Letting
\hat{b}_{\mu}^{(1)}(s,a)
and
\hat{b}_{\mu}^{(2)}(s,a)
be the components of
\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{(1)}
and
\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{(2)}
(Appendix
A.7.2
) corresponding to
(s,a)
, we have

\displaystyle\hat{b}_{\mu}^{(1)}(s,a)=\frac{N_{1}(s,a)}{\lfloor N/2\rfloor}\quad

\displaystyle\&\quad\hat{b}_{\mu}^{(2)}(s,a)=\frac{N_{2}(s,a)}{N-\lfloor N/2\rfloor},

\displaystyle\text{with }N_{1}(s,a)=\sum_{i=1}^{\lfloor N/2\rfloor}\mathbf{1}\big\{(S_{i},A_{i})=(s,a)\big\}\quad

\displaystyle\&\quad N_{2}(s,a)=\sum_{i=\lfloor N/2\rfloor+1}^{N}\mathbf{1}\big\{(S_{i},A_{i})=(s,a)\big\}.

Let us first prove Fact 1. Letting
s,a\in\mathcal{S\times A}
be arbitrary, we have the following,

\displaystyle\bigg|\hat{b}_{\mu}^{(1)}(s,a)-b_{\mu}(s,a)\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{s,a}\bigg|\hat{b}_{\mu}^{(1)}(s,a)-b_{\mu}(s,a)\bigg|=\|\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{(1)}-\mathbf{p}\|_{\infty}\leq\|\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{(1)}-\hat{\mathbf{p}}\|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}p_{{\rm min}}\cdot\epsilon\leq\frac{1}{2}b_{\mu}(s,a)\ \ (\because\epsilon<1).

With the same logic, we also have
|\hat{b}_{\mu}^{(2)}(s,a)-b_{\mu}(s,a)|\leq\frac{1}{2}b_{\mu}(s,a)
, and thereby
\hat{b}_{\mu}^{(1)}(s,a),\hat{b}_{\mu}^{(2)}(s,a)\in[\frac{1}{2}b_{\mu}(s,a),\frac{3}{2}b_{\mu}(s,a)]

which validates Fact 1.
Fact 2 can be validated, since we have
N_{1}(s,a),N_{2}(s,a)\geq 1
and
p_{\rm min}>0
.
Showing Fact 3 is straightforward from (
29
).

C.1.4
Proof Of Lemma
A.2

Let us temporarily assume that
N\in\mathbb{N}
is an even number. Newly define
Y_{ij}:=(X_{ij}+X_{ji})/2
for
1\leq i<j\leq N
. Then we can apply the trick that we used in (
33
) of defining
N-1
groups
G_{1},\cdots,G_{N-1}
and applying Lemma S4 of
Wang
et al. 2022
, which leads to

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigg\{\bigg|\frac{1}{N(N-1)}\sum_{i\neq j}X_{ij}-\mathbb{E}(X_{12})\bigg|\geq\epsilon\bigg\}

\displaystyle\leq(N-1)\cdot\mathbb{P}\bigg\{\bigg|\frac{1}{N/2}\sum_{(i,j)\in G_{1}}Y_{ij}-\mathbb{E}(Y_{12})\bigg|\geq\epsilon\bigg\}

\displaystyle\leq 2N\cdot\exp\bigg\{\frac{-C_{2}\cdot N\cdot\epsilon^{2}}{\|X_{12}-\mathbb{E}(X_{12})\|_{\psi_{2}}^{2}}\bigg\}.

Then we can apply the idea used in Appendix
A.7.3
to expand ourselves into odd numbers
N\in\mathbb{N}
. Eventually we obtain the desired result for an arbitrary integer
N\in\mathbb{N}
.

C.1.5
Proof For Lemma
A.3

It is trivial for
\epsilon>1
since the probability term in the LHS shall be 0, so we will assume
\epsilon\in(0,1)
. Let
\mathbf{Y}_{i}:=\mathbf{X}_{i}-\mathbf{p}
, and we can see
\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{Y}_{1})=\mathbf{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{H}
, along with

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{Y}_{1}\|=\|\mathbf{X_{1}}-\mathbf{p}\|\leq\|\mathbf{X}_{1}\|+\|\mathbf{p}\|\leq 2\ \ \big(\because\ \|\mathbf{p}\|=\sqrt{\sum_{h=1}^{H}p_{h}^{2}}\leq\sqrt{\sum_{h=1}^{H}p_{h}}=1\big).

Therefore we can let
\mu=2
and
\sigma^{2}=4
, and applying Lemma 18 of
Kohler and
Lucchi 2017
gives us the desired result for
\epsilon\in(0,2)
, so it validates the result for
\epsilon\in(0,1)
.

C.1.6
Proof Of Remark
A.5

Let
t>0
be arbitrarily chosen. Under Assumption
4.1
,
\|\theta_{1}-\theta_{2}\|\leq t/L
implies
\mathbb{W}_{1,\infty}(\theta_{1},\theta_{2})\leq t
. Letting
M_{0}=\mathcal{N}(\Theta,\|\cdot\|,t/L)
defined under
A.7
, and
\theta_{1},\cdots,\theta_{M_{0}}
to be such centers, we have

\displaystyle\Theta\subset\bigcup_{i=1}^{M_{0}}N_{\|\cdot\|}(\theta_{i},t/L)\subset\bigcup_{i=1}^{M_{0}}N_{\mathbb{W}_{1,\infty}}(\theta_{i},t),\ \ \ \ \therefore\ \mathcal{N}(\Theta,\mathbb{W}_{1,\infty},t)\leq M_{0}=\mathcal{N}(\Theta,\|\cdot\|,t/L),

which leads to

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\sqrt{\log\mathcal{N}(\Theta,\mathbb{W}_{1,\infty},t)}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}\sqrt{\log\mathcal{N}(\Theta,\|\cdot\|,t/L)}\mathrm{d}t\leq L\cdot\int_{0}^{{\rm diam}(\Theta;\|\cdot\|)}\sqrt{\log\mathcal{N}(\Theta,\|\cdot\|,t)}\mathrm{d}t,

where the last line holds since we have
\mathcal{N}(\Theta,\|\cdot\|,t)=1
for
\forall t\geq{\rm diam}(\Theta;\|\cdot\|)
. Here we can use the well-known Volume Comparison Lemma
that enables us to take up as follows,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\sqrt{\log\mathcal{N}(\Theta,\mathbb{W}_{1,\infty},t)}\mathrm{d}t\leq L\cdot\int_{0}^{{\rm diam}(\Theta;\|\cdot\|)}\sqrt{p\cdot\log\bigg\{1+\frac{2\cdot{\rm diam}(\Theta;\|\cdot\|)}{t}\bigg\}}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq L\cdot\int_{0}^{{\rm diam}(\Theta;\|\cdot\|)}\sqrt{p\cdot\log\bigg\{\frac{3\cdot{\rm diam}(\Theta;\|\cdot\|)}{t}\bigg\}}\mathrm{d}t\leq 6\sqrt{2\pi}\cdot L\sqrt{p}\cdot{\rm diam}(\Theta;\|\cdot\|).

C.1.7
Proof Of Lemma
B.2

Letting
\mu_{0},\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\in\mathcal{P}
be arbitrary, we have
\bar{\mathcal{E}}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})\leq(\text{MMD}(\mu_{0},\mu_{2})+\text{MMD}(\mu_{2},\mu_{1}))^{2}
that leads to following,

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{E}(\mu_{0},\mu_{2})+\sqrt{\mathcal{E}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}\cdot\big\{2\sqrt{\mathcal{E}(\mu_{0},\mu_{2})}+\sqrt{\mathcal{E}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}\big\}.

Now let us extend this result towards
\bar{\mathcal{E}}
as follows,

\displaystyle\bar{\mathcal{E}}(\Upsilon_{0},\Upsilon_{1})\leq\bar{\mathcal{E}}(\Upsilon_{0},\Upsilon_{2})+\sum_{s,a}\bigg[\sqrt{b_{\mu}(s,a)\cdot\mathcal{E}\{\Upsilon_{1}(s,a),\Upsilon_{2}(s,a)\}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\sqrt{b_{\mu}(s,a)}\cdot\bigg\{2\sqrt{\mathcal{E}\{\Upsilon_{0}(s,a),\Upsilon_{2}(s,a)\}}+\sqrt{\mathcal{E}\{\Upsilon_{1}(s,a),\Upsilon_{2}(s,a)\}}\bigg\}\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq\bar{\mathcal{E}}(\Upsilon_{0},\Upsilon_{2})+\bigg(\sum_{s,a}b_{\mu}(s,a)\cdot\mathcal{E}\{\Upsilon_{1}(s,a),\Upsilon_{2}(s,a)\}\bigg)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\bigg[\sum_{s,a}b_{\mu}(s,a)\cdot\bigg\{2\sqrt{\mathcal{E}\{\Upsilon_{0}(s,a),\Upsilon_{2}(s,a)\}}+\sqrt{\mathcal{E}\{\Upsilon_{1}(s,a),\Upsilon_{2}(s,a)\}}\bigg\}^{2}\bigg]^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq\bar{\mathcal{E}}(\Upsilon_{0},\Upsilon_{2})+4\cdot\bar{\mathcal{E}}(\Upsilon_{1},\Upsilon_{2})^{1/2}\cdot\big\{\bar{\mathcal{E}}(\Upsilon_{0},\Upsilon_{2})+\bar{\mathcal{E}}(\Upsilon_{1},\Upsilon_{2})\big\}^{1/2},

where the second inequality is based on Cauchy-Schwartz inequality, the last line is based on
(x+y)^{2}\leq 2(x^{2}+y^{2})
.
Similarly, we have
\bar{\mathcal{E}}(\Upsilon_{0},\Upsilon_{2})\leq\bar{\mathcal{E}}(\Upsilon_{0},\Upsilon_{1})+4\cdot\bar{\mathcal{E}}(\Upsilon_{1},\Upsilon_{2})^{1/2}\cdot\{\bar{\mathcal{E}}(\Upsilon_{0},\Upsilon_{1})+\bar{\mathcal{E}}(\Upsilon_{1},\Upsilon_{2})\}^{1/2}.

This eventually leads to

\displaystyle\bigg|\bar{\mathcal{E}}(\Upsilon_{0},\Upsilon_{1})-\bar{\mathcal{E}}(\Upsilon_{0},\Upsilon_{2})\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq 4\cdot\bar{\mathcal{E}}(\Upsilon_{1},\Upsilon_{2})^{1/2}\cdot\bigg[\max\bigg\{\bar{\mathcal{E}}(\Upsilon_{0},\Upsilon_{1}),\bar{\mathcal{E}}(\Upsilon_{0},\Upsilon_{2})\bigg\}+\bar{\mathcal{E}}(\Upsilon_{1},\Upsilon_{2})\bigg]^{1/2}.

C.1.8
Proof Of Lemma
B.4

Let
z>0
be arbitrary, and let
E_{i}=X_{i}\cdot\mathbf{1}\big\{|X_{i}|\leq z\big\}
and
F_{i}=X_{i}\cdot\mathbf{1}\big\{|X_{i}|>z\big\}
. Since we have
X_{i}=E_{i}+F_{i}
, we have
\mathbb{E}|\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}|\leq\mathbb{E}|\sum_{i=1}^{n}E_{i}|+\mathbb{E}|\sum_{i=1}^{n}F_{i}|
.
Note that each term satisfies the following due to
\mathbb{E}(E_{1})=0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg|\sum_{i=1}^{n}E_{i}\bigg|\leq\mathbb{E}\bigg\{\big(\sum_{i=1}^{n}E_{i}\big)^{2}\bigg\}^{1/2}=\sqrt{\mathbb{V}\bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{n}E_{i}\bigg)}\leq\sqrt{n}\cdot\mathbb{V}(E_{1})^{1/2}=\sqrt{n}\cdot\big[\mathbb{E}\big\{X_{1}^{2}\cdot\mathbf{1}\big(|X_{1}|\leq z\big)\big\}\big]^{1/2}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg|\sum_{i=1}^{n}V_{i}\bigg|\leq n\cdot\mathbb{E}|V_{1}|=n\cdot\mathbb{E}\big\{|X_{1}|\cdot\mathbf{1}\big(|X_{1}|>z\big)\big\},

\displaystyle\therefore\ \mathbb{E}\big|\bar{X}_{n}\big|\leq\inf_{z>0}\bigg[\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\cdot\bigg\{\mathbb{E}\big\{X_{1}^{2}\cdot\mathbf{1}(|X_{1}|\leq z)\big\}\bigg\}^{1/2}+\mathbb{E}\big\{|X_{1}|\cdot\mathbf{1}(|X_{1}|>z)\big\}\bigg]\rightarrow 0\ \ \text{as }n\rightarrow\infty.

C.1.9
Proof Of Equation (
67
)

With
F_{m}(\cdot)
and
F(\cdot)
defined in (
17
), we we have

\displaystyle\big|F_{m}(\theta)-F(\theta)\big|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{s,a}b_{\mu}(s,a)\cdot\bigg|\mathcal{E}\bigg\{\Upsilon_{\theta}(s,a),(\mathcal{T}^{\pi})^{m}\Upsilon_{\theta}(s,a)\bigg\}-\mathcal{E}\bigg\{\Upsilon_{\theta}(s,a),\Upsilon_{\pi}(s,a)\bigg\}\bigg|.

We can further bound it by using the technique shown in Line (
53
). With abuse of notation
(\mathcal{T}^{\pi})^{m}\theta
introduced in Definition (
56
), we can derive the following by the trick used in (
53
) and Assumption
4.1
,

\displaystyle\bigg|\mathcal{E}\bigg\{\Upsilon_{\theta}(s,a),(\mathcal{T}^{\pi})^{m}\Upsilon_{\theta}(s,a)\bigg\}-\mathcal{E}\bigg\{\Upsilon_{\theta}(s,a),\Upsilon_{\pi}(s,a)\bigg\}\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq 4\gamma^{m}\cdot\big\{\mathbb{W}_{1,\infty}(\tilde{\theta},\pi)+\sup_{\theta}\mathbb{W}_{1,\infty}(\theta,\tilde{\theta})\big\}

\displaystyle\leq 4\gamma^{m}\cdot\big\{\mathbb{W}_{1,\infty}(\tilde{\theta},\pi)+L\cdot{\rm diam}(\Theta;\|\cdot\|)\big\}.

C.1.10
Proof Of Remark
B.6

Let us show the first fact.
\psi^{-1}(\cdot)
is an increasing function, since the following holds for arbitrary
y_{1},y_{2}
(
y_{1}\leq y_{2}
),

\displaystyle\psi^{-1}(y_{1})=\inf_{\delta>0}\big\{\psi(\delta)\geq y_{1}\big\}\leq\inf_{\delta>0}\big\{\psi(\delta)\geq y_{2}\big\}=\psi^{-1}(y_{2}).

If
y>\sup_{\delta>0}\psi(\delta)
, then
\psi^{-1}(y)=\inf(\varnothing):=\infty
by definition of infimum. To prove
\lim_{y\rightarrow 0}\psi^{-1}(y)=0
, it suffices to show right-side convergence. Towards that end, we let
\delta>0
be sufficiently small, that is
\delta<\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\|\theta-\tilde{\theta}\|
, which leads to following by Definition (
68
) and Assumption
B.5
(with general
q\geq 1
),

\displaystyle\psi(\delta)=\inf_{\theta\in\Theta:\|\theta-\tilde{\theta}\|\geq\delta}F(\theta)-F(\tilde{\theta})\geq c_{q}\cdot\delta^{q},\quad\therefore\ \psi^{-1}(y)\leq\frac{1}{{c_{q}}^{1/q}}\cdot y^{1/q}\ \ \ \text{for }\forall y\in[0,\sup_{\delta>0}\psi(\delta)).

(87)

This gives us
\psi^{-1}(y)\rightarrow 0
as
y\rightarrow 0
.

The second fact can be shown as follows. Let
y\in[0,\psi(\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\|\theta-\tilde{\theta}\|)]
be arbitrary (
\psi^{-1}(y)<\infty
), and let
\epsilon>0
be arbitrarily small. Letting
\delta_{0}:=\inf_{\delta>0}\big\{\psi(\delta)\geq y\big\}
, we have following,

\displaystyle\psi\big\{\psi^{-1}(y)+\epsilon\big\}=\psi\bigg[\inf_{\delta>0}\bigg\{\psi(\delta)\geq y\bigg\}+\epsilon\bigg]=\psi(\delta_{0}+\epsilon)\geq y,\quad\therefore\ \lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0+}\psi\big\{\psi^{-1}(y)+\epsilon\big\}\geq y.

The third fact can be validated by extending the proof of Example 1.3 suggested by
Sen 2018
. Suppose that there exists a minimizer
\hat{\theta}\in\arg\min_{\theta\in\Theta}\hat{F}(\theta)
such that
\|\hat{\theta}-\theta_{0}\|\geq\delta
. Now we temporarily make new notations
G=-F
and
\hat{G}=-\hat{F}
, which leads to
\hat{\theta}\in\arg\max_{\theta\in\Theta}\hat{G}(\theta)
and
\psi(\delta)=G(\tilde{\theta})-\sup_{\theta\in\Theta:\|\theta-\tilde{\theta}\|\geq\delta}G(\theta)
. Then we have
\hat{G}(\tilde{\theta})\leq\sup_{\theta\in\Theta:\|\theta-\tilde{\theta}\|\geq\delta}\hat{G}(\theta)
, which leads to

\displaystyle\sup_{\theta\in\Theta:\|\theta-\tilde{\theta}\|\geq\delta}\hat{G}(\theta)-\hat{G}(\tilde{\theta})+\psi(\delta)\geq\psi(\delta),\quad\therefore\ \sup_{\theta\in\Theta:\|\theta-\tilde{\theta}\|\geq\delta}\big\{(\hat{G}(\theta)-G(\theta))-(\hat{G}(\theta)-G(\tilde{\theta}))\big\}\geq\psi(\delta),

from which we can derive
\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}|\hat{F}(\theta)-F(\theta)|\geq\frac{1}{2}\psi(\delta)
. Up to this point we have derived

\displaystyle\exists\hat{\theta}\in\arg\min_{\theta\in\Theta}\hat{F}(\theta)\ \text{such that }\|\hat{\theta}-\theta_{0}\|\geq\delta\quad\rightarrow\quad\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}|\hat{F}(\theta)-F(\theta)|\geq\frac{1}{2}\psi(\delta).

Now let us assume that there exists a value
\hat{\theta}\in\Theta
such that
\|\hat{\theta}-\tilde{\theta}\|>\delta
. Then we have
\|\hat{\theta}-\tilde{\theta}\|=\delta+\epsilon_{0}
for some
\epsilon_{0}>0
.
By above statement,
we have
\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}|\hat{F}(\theta)-F(\theta)|\geq\frac{1}{2}\psi(\delta+\epsilon_{0})\geq\frac{1}{2}\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0+}\psi(\delta+\epsilon)
, by using the fact that
\psi(\cdot)
is an increasing function. This gives us the desired result.

C.1.11
Continuity Of Bootstrap-based Objective Function

Note that
\mathcal{B}_{m}
can also be viewed as a Bellman operator corresponding to a different transition probability
\hat{p}_{m}^{(B)}
(
51
). That being said, by replicating the trick used in (
53
) and Assumption
4.1
, we have

\displaystyle\bigg|\mathcal{E}\bigg\{

\displaystyle\Upsilon_{\theta_{1}}(s,a),\mathcal{B}_{m}\Upsilon_{\theta_{1}}(s,a)\bigg\}-\mathcal{E}\bigg\{\Upsilon_{\theta_{2}}(s,a),\mathcal{B}_{m}\Upsilon_{\theta_{2}}(s,a)\bigg\}\bigg|\leq 8L\cdot\|\theta_{1}-\theta_{2}\|,

which further leads to
|\hat{F}_{m}^{(B)}(\theta_{1})-\hat{F}_{m}^{(B)}(\theta_{2})|\leq 8L\cdot\|\theta_{1}-\theta_{2}\|
, implying Lipschitz continuity of the objective function (
18
).

C.2
Further Discussion

C.2.1
Theorem
2.2
Is Not Trivial For General State-action Space

As we warned below Theorem
2.2
, Theorem
2.2
can be trivial in tabular setting
|\mathcal{S\times A}|<\infty
, but this is not trivial for non-tabular setting
|\mathcal{S\times A}|\geq\infty
. This is analogous to the fact that
L_{2}
-norm and
L_{\infty}
-norm are not equivalent for infinite-dimensional objects (e.g. functional objects).
For a function
g:\mathcal{S\times A}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, let us define
\|g\|_{2}:=\sqrt{E(g(S,A)^{2})}
and
\|g\|_{\infty}:=\sup_{s,a}|g|
. Assuming
g(s,a)=e^{-s}
,
(S,A)\sim Uniform([0,1]^{2})
leads to bounded
L_{2}
-norm
\|g\|_{2}=\sqrt{1-e^{-1}}<\infty
, yet we still have unbounded
L_{\infty}
-norm
\|g\|_{\infty}=\infty
.

First, let us assume tabular setting. Let
p_{min}
be the minimum (or infimum) value of all
b_{\mu}(s,a)
where
b_{\mu}
is the density value at the given state-action pair. If we are given with a tabular setting where the underlying measure of the state-action space is the count measure, we have
b_{\mu}(s,a)=\mathbb{P}(S,A=s,a)
. Due to
b_{\mu}(s,a)/p_{min}\geq 1
, the following holds for
\eta_{\infty}(\Upsilon_{1},\Upsilon_{2}):=\sup_{s,a}\eta(\Upsilon_{1}(s,a),\Upsilon_{2}(s,a))
,

\displaystyle\eta_{\infty}(\Upsilon_{1},\Upsilon_{2})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{s,a}\eta\{\Upsilon_{1}(s,a),\Upsilon_{2}(s,a)\}\leq\sum_{s,a}\frac{b_{\mu}(s,a)}{p_{min}}\cdot\eta\{\Upsilon_{1}(s,a),\Upsilon_{2}(s,a)\}=\frac{1}{p_{min}}\cdot\bar{\eta}(\Upsilon_{1},\Upsilon_{2}).

Then, we can make use of this to bound the inaccuracy with Bellman residual as follows,

\displaystyle\bar{\eta}(\Upsilon,\Upsilon_{\pi})\leq\eta_{\infty}(\Upsilon,\Upsilon_{\pi})\leq\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{\beta_{0}}}\cdot\eta_{\infty}(\Upsilon,\mathcal{T}^{\pi}\Upsilon)\leq\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{\beta_{0}}}\cdot\frac{1}{p_{min}}\cdot\bar{\eta}(\Upsilon,\mathcal{T}^{\pi}\Upsilon),

where the second inequality is due to Equation (
4
).

However, we cannot apply the same logic to state-action space with infinite cardinality
|\mathcal{S\times A}|\geq\infty
. Assume continuous state-action space, for example. In this case, it is tempting to switch all the summation
\sum_{s,a}
into integral
\int_{\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}}
. Unfortunately, however, we cannot show
\eta_{\infty}(\Upsilon_{1},\Upsilon_{2})\leq\int_{\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}}\eta\{\Upsilon_{1}(s,a),\Upsilon_{2}(s,a)\}\mathrm{d}m(s,a)
, where
m
is the underlying measure of the state-action space (say, Lesbesgue measure). Even when the space is countable (which allows us to use discrete summation
\sum_{s,a}
), we still have an issue since the infimum value of the density will be
p_{min}=0
, which leads to infinite-bound on the RHS.

In conclusion, the expectation-extended distance and supremum-extended distance are not equivalent when state-action space has infinite cardinality (i.e. non-tabular state-action space). This tells us that Theorem
2.2
is not trivial, since this can be applied for non-tabular state-action space (with either countable or uncountable cardinality). The well-known Atari games is nearly a non-tabular example with extremely many state-action pairs (state space alone having huge cardinality
|\mathcal{S}|=256\times 250\times 160\times 3
which has little difference with
\infty
at least in the practical sense). The OpenAI-gym classical games of Section
5.1
is an example which has continuous state space (with uncountably infinite cardinality). These two examples can be supported by our Theorem
A.7
, which was built upon Theorem
2.2
, since they have deterministic transition.

C.2.2
Model Misspecification Example

Equation (
16
) can be illustrated schematically as follows.

Figure 2:
Larger
m
makes
(\mathcal{T}^{\pi})^{m}\Upsilon_{\theta}\approx\Upsilon_{\pi}
in expected energy distance, and thereby leads to
\theta_{*}^{(m)}\approx\tilde{\theta}
.

Here is an example. Letting
\rho=\theta
, we assume an arbitrarily large state-action space
|\mathcal{S\times A}|\in\mathbb{N}
, with an arbitrary behavior policy
b
and target policy
\pi
. We assume that rewards are 2-dimensional (
d=2
), and follow the same distribution conditioned on any state-action pair,

\displaystyle R(s,a)\sim N(\mathbf{0}_{2},\Sigma(\rho_{0}))\quad\text{where}\quad\Sigma(\rho):=\sigma_{0}^{2}\cdot\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&\rho\\
\rho&1\end{array}\right),\quad\therefore\ Z_{\pi}(s,a)\sim N(\mathbf{0}_{2},\Sigma(\rho_{0})/(1-\gamma^{2})).

Figure 3:
At the top, red line represents the selected minimizer of each
F_{m}
by R function
optimize
, which may be a non-global local minimizer, whereas the green line represents the best
\bar{\mathcal{E}}
-approximation. The bottom plots represent the corresponding pdf contour plots.

With
\gamma=0.99
, we assume that the true values are
\sigma_{0}^{2}=4
and
\rho_{0}=0.5
, but we misspecified the variance with
\sigma_{1}^{2}=10
, and then we let
\rho\in[-1,1]
to be the only parameter of interest. In this case,
\tilde{\rho}\approx 0.776
gives us the best
\bar{\mathcal{E}}
-approximation, being the minimizer of the inaccuracy function
F(\rho)
(
17
). We can see in Figure
3
that the minimizers of the population objective functions
F_{m}(\rho)
approaches
\tilde{\rho}
as we increase
m\rightarrow\infty
. Moreover, in this example, as
F_{m}
uniformly converges to
F
(
15
), the risk of selecting the non-global local minimizer is reduced. However, we can also see in the rightmost bottom plot that there is irreducible error from the true distribution, due to misspecification of the variance value
\sigma_{1}^{2}>\sigma_{0}^{2}
.

C.2.3
Examples That Satisfies Assumption
B.5
With Quadratic Bound

We will suggest two examples: strongly-convex and non-convex functions
F(\theta)
, which satisfy Assumption
B.5
with
q=2
.

Here is a strongly convex example. We can arbitrarily choose the cardinality
|\mathcal{S\times A}|>0
and discount rate
\gamma\in(0,1)
. We can also assume any behavior and target policies
b
and
\pi
. Conditioned on
s,a
, its reward follows
R(s,a)\sim N(0,\tilde{\theta})
with
\tilde{\theta}>0
, which leads the target distribution
\Upsilon_{\pi}(s,a)
to follow
N(0,\tilde{\theta}/(1-\gamma^{2}))
at all
s,a\in\mathcal{S\times A}
. We select a model so that
\Upsilon_{\theta}(s,a)
is the probability measure of
N(0,\theta/(1-\gamma^{2}))
with
\theta\in\Theta\subset[0,\infty)
for all
s,a\in\mathcal{S\times A}
. Then we have

\displaystyle F(\theta)

\displaystyle=\bar{\mathcal{E}}(\Upsilon_{\theta},\Upsilon_{\pi})=\sum_{s,a}b_{\mu}(s,a)\cdot\mathcal{E}\{\Upsilon_{\theta}(s,a),\Upsilon_{\pi}(s,a)\}=\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-\gamma^{2}}}\cdot(\sqrt{2(\theta+\tilde{\theta})}-\sqrt{\theta}-\sqrt{\tilde{\theta}}).

For simplicity, let us assume
\tilde{\theta}=1
and
\Theta=[0,2]
. Since we have
F^{\prime}(1)=0
and
F^{\prime\prime}(1)=\frac{1}{4}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{1}{\pi\cdot(1-\gamma^{2})}}>0
, this satisfies Assumption
B.5
with
q=2
.

Next, we will suggest a non-convex example of
F(\mu)
that may have multiple local minimizers. We will skip detailed calculations. Assume that the reward follows
R(s,a)\sim N(\mu_{*},\sigma_{0}^{2})
with known value of
\sigma_{0}^{2}
at all
s,a
, which leads to the true return distribution
Z_{\pi}(s,a)\sim N(\mu_{*}/(1-\gamma),\sigma_{0}^{2}/(1-\gamma^{2}))
. Let us model our candidate distributions with
\mu\in[a,b]
so that
Z(s,a;\mu)\sim N(g(\mu)/(1-\gamma),\sigma_{0}^{2}/(1-\gamma^{2}))
with a pre-determined function
g
. Although letting
g(\mu)=\mu
makes
F(\cdot)
strongly convex, we may want to include various
g
. Relaxing
g
into a non-monotonic function will lead to non-convex
F
(possibly with local minimizers), however it satisfies Assumption 4.2 as long as following hold:

(i) There exists a unique value (say
\mu_{0}
) that satisfies
g(\mu_{0})=\mu_{*}
,

(ii)
g(\cdot)
is a straight line segment in a neighborhood of
\mu_{0}
.

Note that (ii) can be relaxed into a polynomial curve instead of straight line segment, which can lead us to resort to more generalized Assumption
B.5
that yields slower convergence rate (Theorem
B.7
).

While early TD (temporal difference) methods of DRL demonstrated outperformance in Atari games over traditional deep-Q learning
(
Mnih
et al. 2015
)
, they do not have sound theoretical foundation. Instead of proving convergence or deriving statistical error bound, they mostly end up showing contraction of operators with respect to some supremum-extended (
3
) probability distances. These operators are mostly Bellman operators or Bellman operators followed by some projection (e.g.
\Pi_{\mathcal{C}},\Pi_{\mathbb{W}_{1}},\mathcal{O}_{c}:\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathcal{S\times A}}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathcal{S\times A}}
). Refer to
Rowland et al. 2018
,
Dabney
et al. 2018
,
Ma
et al. 2021
for their exact definitions.

Of course, showing this contraction is not sufficient for showing probabilistic convergence. Exact Bellman-update is not even possible, due to random transition and tremendously many state-action pairs. This is why they use functional approximation (e.g. deep neural network) and minimize an objective function instead. In order to theoretically justify their theoretical convergence, by (
4
), they should show that their algorithm minimizes supremum-extended Bellman residual
\eta_{\infty}(\Upsilon,\mathcal{T}^{\pi}\Upsilon)
. Here,
\eta_{\infty}
is the supremum-extended metric that they showed contraction of their operator. However, as can be seen from Table
4
, the objective functions that they have used do not seem to have any relationship with
\eta_{\infty}
that can lead to minimizing the term
\eta_{\infty}(\Upsilon,\mathcal{T}^{\pi}\Upsilon)
.

Table 4:

: Wasserstein-
p
(
p\geq 1
)

(supremum-extended)

(

et al. 2017
)

: Cramer distance

(

)

(supremum-extended)

(

)

: Wasserstein-

)

(supremum-extended)

(

)

Following the aforementioned methods, further works are done to verify statistical convergence in infinite-horizontal settings (Table
5
). However, many of them are restricted in many aspects. For instance, most of them only showed consistency without any statistical error bound (or probability convergence rate). Even in such cases, many are based on strong restrictions. These include (i) tabular setting (
|\mathcal{S\times A}|<\infty
), (ii) bounded rewards, (iii) availability of generative model (i.e. known transition probability
p(r,s^{\prime}|s,a)
). In this regard, FLE
(
Wu
et al. 2023
)
is a great achievement in that it was free from all these restrictions. However, it was still based on a very strong condition called “completeness” that we have explained in Section
1
.

(Theorem 1, Proposition 4)

algorithm

(

)

(

et al. 2023
)

(Tabular case, no convergence rate)

(

)

(Tabular case, bounded reward, ignores model restriction)

NTD

shows finite-sample error bound (Theorem 3.1)

(

et al. 2024
)

(tabular case, requires generative model)

Convergence rate suggested (Corollary 4.14)

(

)

(bounded reward, requires completeness)

3.3
, Appendix

D.2
OpenAI-gym Classical Games Simulation

D.2.1
Simulation Settings

In all three methods (EBRM, QRDQN, MDMQN), we used the following network structure and tuning parameters. In three games, the state spaces are
\mathcal{S}\subsetneq\mathbb{R}^{p}
with
p=6,4,2
and the numbers of actions are
|\mathcal{A}|=3,2,3
for Acrobot, Cartpole and Mountaincar, respectively. We are using 5 particles (or quantiles) to estimate the conditional distributions.

Table 6:
Algorithmic details

Tuning parameters

Value

layers and nodes

FC (
\text{dim}(\mathcal{S})\rightarrow 10
), ReLU, FC (
10\rightarrow|\mathcal{A}|\times 5
)

Number of particles

5

Learning Rate

0.01

optimizer

Adam

OpenAI-gym games are originally designed as finite-horizontal settings where the game ends once the agent reaches the terminal state. However, we adjusted the game into infinite-horizontal setting by letting the agent return to a fixed point once it reaches the terminal state. We let the reward at the terminal state to be different from other rewards. Sample size is fixed as 100, where samples are collected based on a trajectory of
(s,a,r,s^{\prime})
through uniform policy
b(a|s)=1/|\mathcal{A}|
and some initial state distribution
s\sim\mu
.

Since we are estimating the distribution of true return variable
Z_{\pi}(s,a)\sim\Upsilon_{\pi}(s,a)
that is “random,” we let our target policy to be random. Our target policy was epsilon-greedy policy of the optimal policy that we trained with DQN
(
Mnih
et al. 2015
)
. That is, with probability
1-\epsilon
, the agent selects the optimal action learned by DQN, and with probability
\epsilon
, it randomly selects an action with equal probability.

However,
\epsilon
-greedy policy alone does not make
Z_{\pi}(s,a)
random. Since it takes a long while to reach the terminal state and the rewards in non-terminal states are the same, the return becomes non-random for most of the times. Therefore, we let the agent repeat the selected action for multiple times, and consider this as a single transition. The settings are as follows for each game.

Table 7:
Details of experimental setting (OpenAI-gym games)

Acrobot

Cartpole

Mountaincar

reward at non-terminal state

-1

1

-1

reward at terminal state

5

-5

2

epsilon

0.1

0.9

0.3

repetition of each action

10

1

10

We used energy distance and Wasserstein metric to assess the inaccuracy from the true distribution that we approximated with Monte Carlo simulations. However, since there are too many state-action pairs, it is computationally impossible to approximate
\Upsilon_{\pi}(s,a)
for all
s,a
. Therefore, we evaluated the marginal distributions instead, according to Corollary 4.14 of
Wu
et al. 2023
, as we have mentioned in Section
5.1
.

D.2.2
More Extensive Simulations On Cartpole Game

Cartpole games seemed to best satisfy realizability assumption among all three games and have been tried by many other papers in reinforcement learning
(
Wang
et al. 2023
;
Narita
et al. 2021
, e.g.,)
. Therefore, we have done more extensive simulations on Cartpole games, based on various sample sizes and larger neural networks. In all three methods (EBRM, QRDQN, MDMQN), we used the following network structures (small and big) and tuning parameters (Table
8
). In Cartpole, we have
\mathcal{S}\subsetneq\mathbb{R}^{4}
(which means
\text{dim}(\mathcal{S})=4
) and 2 actions (
|\mathcal{A}|=2
).

The way we have constructed the settings is basically the same with that of Appendix
D.2.1
. However, we have tried more various sample sizes (ranging from 100 to 1000) and made further adjustments in the settings. We have tried three different settings (Table
9
), where each setting gives us different types of marginal return distributions: left-skewed (Setting 1), bell-shaped (Setting 2), and highly-concentrated (Setting 3) marginal distributions. These are visualized in Figure
4
.

Simulations are done in both neural networks shown in Table
8
, whose results are shown in each of Tables
10
–
15
, and visualized in Figure
5
. Outperformance of EBRM is evident in both accuracy and stability, particularly when equipped with the larger network model. This aligns with Theorem
A.7
, since larger models will lead to smaller extent of non-realizability.

D.3
Realizable And Nonrealizable Models

D.3.1
Introduction Of Simulation Settings

The initial state distribution and behavior / target policies are

\displaystyle S\sim\text{Unif}\big\{1,2,\cdots,30\big\}\quad\mbox{and}\quad A\sim b(\cdot|S),

(88)

\displaystyle b(a|s)=1/2\quad\mbox{and}\quad\pi(1|s)=1\quad\mbox{for}\quad\forall s,a\in\mathcal{S\times A}.

Let us assume that
s,a
is given. The agent moves by one in the direction of
a\in\{-1,1\}
, that is
s^{\prime}=s+a
(value of which fully determines the reward distribution (
89
)). If the agent is already blocked by the direction, that is
(s,a)=(1,-1)
or
(s,a)=(30,1)
, it stays at the same position
s^{\prime}=s
. With given values of
A_{0}>0,p_{0}\in(0,1),\sigma_{0}^{2}>0
, our transition
p(r,s^{\prime}|s,a)
is characterized by:

\displaystyle\text{Conditioned on }S+A=k,\

\displaystyle R\sim N(\mu_{k},\sigma_{0}^{2})\quad\text{where}\quad\mu_{k}=\begin{cases}A_{0}\cdot p_{0}^{k}&(k=0,\cdots,30)\\
0&(k=31)\end{cases}

(89)

and

\displaystyle S^{\prime}=k\ \text{if }k\in\{1,\cdots,30\},\quad S^{\prime}=30\ \text{if }k=31,\quad S^{\prime}=1\ \text{if }k=0.

We assume infinite-horizontal setting. Following the environment (
89
) and target policy (
88
), we have
Z_{\pi}(s,a)\sim\Upsilon_{\pi}(s,a)
to be normal distributions, with expectation and variance as follows,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big\{Z_{\pi}(i,-1)\big\}=A_{0}\cdot p_{0}^{i-1}\cdot\frac{1-(\gamma p_{0})^{32-i}}{1-\gamma p_{0}}\ \ (i\geq 2),\quad\mathbb{V}\big\{Z_{\pi}(i,\pm 1)\big\}=\frac{\sigma_{0}^{2}}{1-\gamma^{2}}\ \ (i\geq 1),

(90)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big\{Z_{\pi}(i,1)\big\}=A_{0}\cdot p_{0}^{i+1}\cdot\frac{1-(\gamma p_{0})^{30-i}}{1-\gamma p_{0}}\ \ (i\geq 1),\quad\mathbb{E}\big\{Z_{\pi}(1,-1)\big\}=A_{0}+\gamma\cdot\mathbb{E}\big\{Z_{\pi}(1,1)\big\}.

We always let
A_{0}=100
,
p_{0}=0.9
throughout the simulations.

First, we assumed a realizable scenario where the correct model (
90
) is known (Appendix
D.3.2
), only not knowing the values of
A_{0}
,
p_{0}
. Here, we always assumed
\gamma=0.99
and tried two settings with
\sigma_{0}^{2}=20
and
\sigma_{0}^{2}=5000
. Second, we always tried the non-realizable scenario where there is a model misspecification (
91
), as will be demonstrated in Appendix
D.3.3
. Here, we always assumed
\sigma_{0}^{2}=20
, trying
\gamma=0.50
and
\gamma=0.99
.

D.3.2
Realizable Scenario

In the realizable scenario, we assume that Equations (
90
) are known, except the values of
A_{0}=100
and
p_{0}=0.9
. The distributions can be plotted as Figure
6
, each for
\sigma_{0}^{2}=20
and
\sigma_{0}^{2}=5000
.

Figure 6:
Red and blue represent the distributions of
Z_{\pi}(s,-1)
and
Z_{\pi}(s,1)
respectively. The dots indicate the expectation values and the vertical bars include (Mean
\pm
3
\cdot
SD).

D.3.3
Non-realizable Scenario

On the other hand, in the non-realizable scenario, we assume that we are not aware of the true model (
90
). Instead, we assume that we are only aware of the decreasing trend demonstrated in Figure
6
. That being said, we apply the following linear model that holds for all
1\leq i\leq 30
. with four different parameters
\beta_{L},\beta_{R},\beta_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
,
\sigma^{2}>0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big(Z_{\pi}(i,-1)\big)=\beta_{L}+\beta_{1}\cdot i,\ \ \mathbb{E}\big(Z_{\pi}(i,1)\big)=\beta_{R}+\beta_{1}\cdot i,\ \ \mathbb{V}\big(Z_{\pi}(i,\pm 1)\big)=\frac{\sigma^{2}}{1-\gamma^{2}}.

(91)

This means that the distributions (conditioned on each
s,a
) have common variance, common slope in expectations, but different
y
-intercepts in expectations.

We always assumed
\sigma_{0}^{2}=20
and tried two different settings,
\gamma=0.50
and
\gamma=0.99
. Denoting the parameter as
\theta=(\beta_{L},\beta_{R},\beta_{1},\sigma^{2})
and candidate space as
\Theta=\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{-}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}
, the best approximation value
\tilde{\theta}
that minimizes the inaccuracy
\bar{\mathcal{E}}(\Upsilon_{\theta},\Upsilon_{\pi})
are calculated in Table
16
. They are visualized in Figure
7
(true distributions
\Upsilon_{\pi}
on left and best approximations
\Upsilon_{\tilde{\theta}}
on right).

Table 16:
Best approximation values and minimum inaccuracy

Scenario

\beta_{L}

\sigma^{2}

Minimum
\bar{\mathcal{E}}
-inaccuracy

Z_{\pi}(s,-1)
and

, or
Z(s,-1;\tilde{\theta})
and

, respectively. The dots indicate the expectation values and the vertical bars include (Mean
\pm

\cdot
SD).

D.4
Tuning Parameters Of Each Method

D.4.1
EBRM

Energy distance (
7
) is calculated via numerical integration given the densities of the probability measures. Here is the algorithm of choosing the step level
m
, solely based on the observed data. The basic skeleton is based on SLOPE suggested by
Su
et al. 2020
based on Lepski’s rule
Lepskii 1991
. Starting from large enough
m
, we can decrease it until the intersection of the confidence intervals (formed with multiple bootstraps) becomes a null set.

Algorithm 3

Lepski’s rule of selecting step level
m

1=m_{0}<m_{1}<\cdots m_{K}

Input:

\mathcal{D}=\{(s_{i},a_{i},r_{i},s_{i}^{\prime})\}_{i=1}^{N}
,
J
,
M
,
(m_{1},m_{2},\cdots,m_{K})

Output:

m_{k}

Estimate
\hat{\theta}
with single-step estimation (
13
).

k\leftarrow K+1
,
O_{K+1}\leftarrow[-\infty,\infty]
.

while

O_{k}\neq\varnothing

do

k\leftarrow k-1
.

if

k\neq 0

then

for

j=1,\cdots,J

do

Estimate
\hat{\theta}_{m_{k},j}^{(B)}
with multi-step estimation (
18
) of step level
m=m_{k}
.

Calculate
\hat{e}_{k,j}:=\hat{\bar{\mathcal{E}}}(\Upsilon_{\hat{\theta}},\Upsilon_{\hat{\theta}_{m_{k},j}^{(B)}})
.

end

for

Calculate the sample mean (
\hat{\mu}_{k}
) and variance (
\hat{s}_{k}
) of
\hat{e}_{k,j}
(
j=1,\cdots,J
).

Calculate
I_{k}:=[\hat{\mu}_{k}\pm 1.96\cdot\hat{s}_{k}]
.

O_{k}\leftarrow O_{k+1}\cap I_{k}
.

else

O_{k}\leftarrow\varnothing
.

end

if

end

while

Throughout multiple simulations in each setting (
D.3.2
and
D.3.3
) for each sample size
N
(demonstrated in
D.5
), it is rigorous to pick its own optimal step level
m
. However, since they do not differ significantly, we picked the step level
m
via Algorithm
3
based on the first simulated data, and used the same value of
m
throughout the remaining simulations, in order to save computational burden. We applied the algorithm with
M=N
and
J=50
. Obviously, we had to try larger values of
(m_{1},\cdots,m_{K})
for non-realizable scenario with
\gamma=0.99
than
\gamma=0.50
. However, to avoid numerical issues in integration caused by extremely small
\gamma^{m}
, we limited the choice of step levels into
m\leq 4
(
\gamma=0.50
) and
m\leq 250
(
\gamma=0.99
). The corresponding intervals
I_{k}
are visualized in Figures
8
and
9
, and the selected step level
m_{*}
is specified in Table
17
.

Table 17:

D.4.2
FLE

Wu
et al. 2023
did not officially suggest a rule of selecting the number of partitions
T
and their sizes
|\mathcal{D}_{1}|,\cdots,|\mathcal{D}_{T}|
, so we utilized its asymptotic result (Corollary 4.14 by
Wu
et al. 2023
) to construct the following heuristic rule based on pre-determined values of
N_{0}
,
T_{0}
, and
l>0
,

\displaystyle T(N)\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{let}}\log_{\big({\frac{1}{\gamma}}\big)^{1-\frac{1}{2l}}}\bigg\{\frac{1}{C_{0}}\cdot\bigg(\frac{N}{\log N}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2l}}\bigg\},\quad\mbox{where }C_{0}>0\text{ satisfies }T(N_{0})=T_{0}.

Note that larger value of
l
slows down the increasing speed of
T(N)
. In addition, we prevent the number of partition
T
from becoming too small, we put a lower bound
\tilde{T}
, so that we have
T^{*}(N)=\max\{\tilde{T},\lfloor T(N)\rfloor\}
.
Then each partition has
|\mathcal{D}_{t}|=\lfloor N/T^{*}(N)\rfloor
, but may have some remaining observations when
N
is not divisible by the chosen
T
. In this case, we included all the remaining observations into the last partition
\mathcal{D}_{T}
. The tuning parameters are chosen after multiple numerical experiments, and the following choice (Table
18
) seemed to work best.

Table 18:
Tuning parameters of FLE

Sample size

l

N_{0}

T_{0}

\tilde{T}

D.4.3
QRTD

QRTD
(
Dabney
et al. 2018
)
was originally designed for updating value distributions only conditioned on the state
s\in\mathcal{S}
, not the action
a\in\mathcal{A}
(refer to their Equation (12)). However, we could readily develop it towards the action-value distributions conditioned on
s,a\in\mathcal{S\times A}
. That is, with
\tau_{i}\in[0,1]
(
1\leq i\leq N_{\tau}
) being cdf values and
\theta_{i}(s,a)
being the corresponding quantile value, they model the distribution to be uniform across
\theta_{i}(s,a)
,

\displaystyle Z_{\theta}(s,a)=\frac{1}{N_{\tau}}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{\tau}}\delta_{\theta_{i}(s,a)}.

Analogously to their original version, we update it as follows, whenever a single new observation
(s,a,r,s^{\prime})
is collected,

\displaystyle\text{Sample }i\sim\text{Unif}\big\{1,\cdots,N_{\tau}\big\}

\displaystyle\theta_{i}(s,a)\leftarrow\theta_{i}(s,a)+\alpha_{0}\cdot\{{\tau}_{i}-\mathbf{1}(r+\gamma z^{\prime}<\theta_{i}(s,a))\}\quad\text{where }z^{\prime}\sim Z_{\theta}(s^{\prime},a^{\prime}),\ a^{\prime}\sim\pi(\cdot|s^{\prime}).

The number of quantiles is chosen to be 99, that is
\tau_{i}=i/100

(1\leq i\leq 99)
. As
Dabney
et al. 2018
mentioned, we repeat the above procedure
for multiple times within each iteration, which we let to be the same as
N_{\tau}=99
times in our case. We chose
\alpha_{0}=5
for
\sigma_{0}^{2}=20
and
\alpha_{0}=2
for
\sigma_{0}^{2}=5000
of realizable cases, since they worked fine empirically.

D.5
Simulation Results

As was mentioned in Section
5
, we included three different inaccuracy measures (Tables
19
–
21
). Expectation-extended energy distance
\bar{\mathcal{E}}
is the one that we have used in our main theoretical results (Theorems
3.3
and
B.7
). For the sake of fairness, we also included expectation-extended (
5
) Wasserstein-1 metric
\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{1}
, based on that Wasserstein-1 metric plays an important role in DRL literature (Table
4
). Lastly, we also used
\mathbb{W}_{1}
to compare the marginal distributions
\Upsilon^{marginal}
which is defined as the mixture of
\{\Upsilon(s,a):s,a\in\mathcal{S\times A}\}
with weights
\{b_{\mu}(s,a):s,a\in\mathcal{S\times A}\}
, which was used in Corollary 4.14 by
Wu
et al. 2023
. Of course,
Wu
et al. 2023
used
\pi_{\mu}(s,a)
as weights, however we will use
b_{\mu}(s,a)
for consistency with other inaccuracy measures. Measurement of the second and third inaccuracies using
\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{1}
and
\mathbb{W}_{1}
was approximated via R package
transport
using randomly generated samples.

D.5.2
Non-realizable Scenario

Now we tried non-realizable settings with the misspecified model (
91
) of Section
D.3.3
, based on tuning parameters determined in Tables
17
and
18
. Since the point of this experiment is to see how each method can perform with a misspecified model. we excluded QRTD, which does not have any model assumption on the return distributions. We could see in Table
22
that EBRM approached the minimum possible level of Energy Distance (13.327 for
\gamma=0.50
and 63.216 for
\gamma=0.99
) as we increased the sample size
N
. This forms contrast with FLE that even deteriorated as sample size grows, which we can supposedly attribute to huge violation of completeness that FLE is based upon. Supposedly, the malfunctioning of FLE in Tables
22
–
24
(under
\gamma=0.99
) can be attributed to its completeness assumption (Assumption 4.12 of FLE
(
Wu
et al. 2023
)
).

D.6
Ablation Studies

In order to check the significant impact of choosing the hyperparameter
m
(step-level), we did comparison between multi-step EBRM (where the step level is chosen based on Algorithm
3
) and single-step EBRM (which always uses
m=1
). Since Algorithm
3
gave us
m=1
for most other cases (see Table
17
), we will only do the comparison for non-realizable setting with
\gamma=0.99
. In Table
25
, we could see big difference in performances, which thereby demonstrates the importance of choosing the step-level.

D.7
Convergence Under Non-completeness

As shown in Section
D.5.2
, for non-realizable setting with
\gamma=0.99
, performance of FLE was not only inaccurate, but also deteriorating as we increase the sample size. This is because FLE is based upon a strong assumption called “completeness” that already implies realizability. If realizability is violated, then completeness is of course violated, since completeness is stronger than realizability. For larger discount rate
\gamma
, the extent of non-realizability grows even larger due to the large quantity of return
Z_{\pi}(s,a):=\sum_{t\geq 1}\gamma^{t-1}R_{t}
. This further takes a huge toll in completeness, and can lead to convergence towards a very distant target. This commonly happens for fitted-q-based or TD-based algorithms when completeness is not satisfied (e.g.
Kolter 2011
,
Tsitsiklis and
Van Roy 1996
,
Munos and
Szepesvari 2008
).

The following are the visualizations of the distributions that EBRM and FLE finally converge to, under non-realizable modeling (
91
). In the figures below, the left column shows the true distributions
Z_{\pi}(s,a)
. The middle shows the best
\bar{\mathcal{E}}
-approximations achieved by
\tilde{\theta}
defined in (
14
), which EBRM-multi-step converges to. The right shows the final converging distributions of FLE, which is approximated by using sufficiently large amount of samples and iterations (
T=1000
iterations, with each iteration using
10^{6}
samples). We can observe that EBRM stays close to true distribution of
Z_{\pi}(s,a)
, whereas FLE converges towards a very distant distribution from
Z_{\pi}(s,a)
as
\gamma
grows larger (Figure
10
).

Figure 10:
Red and blue represent the distributions of
Z(s,-1)
and
Z(s,1)
, with dots indicating the expectation values and the vertical bars include (Mean
\pm
3
\cdot
SD). The left column represents true distributions. The middle and right columns represent the final converging distributions of EBRM and FLE.

Computational Details

Throughout all simulations in our paper, we only used CPU, and all simulations did not use over 1GB of memory. Average computation time for a single simulation is as follows.
